  - **THE SAME MISSING ROLE MADE THE UI A DEAD END, which is why nobody had hit the hole.** No partner owned a `Staff` role, so the Team screen's `roles.find(x => x.name === 'Staff')` returned OUR platform row (`scope: 'all'`) and `isSuperAdminRoleName` filtered Staff out of the dropdown — while `Admin` was already filtered as taken, every partner having exactly one. **The dropdown came up with nothing selectable: no partner could add a second operator at all.** One missing role, a dead end on the guided path and an escalation on the API path. `isSuperAdminRoleName` is tenant-aware now.
  - **`resolveAssignableRole(tenantId, name, allowPlatformFallback)` is the ONE resolver** and the platform fallback is **superadmin-only**: an operator may still deliberately assign a platform custom role, nobody else can reach one. Proof that reads in one line — `'SuperAdmin'` on `finwave`: superadmin → *platform fallback (scope all)*, partner admin → **refused**. It does not replace the `isSuperAdminRole` check at each call site; it removes the easy route to one.
  - **PARTNER STAFF IS SMALLER THAN OUR STAFF, and `scope: 'own'` not `subtree`** (operator: *"partner Staff is less compare to our staff and shown only own not all other"*). `TENANT_STAFF_PERMS` is 6 read-only perms against our internal Staff's **scope `all` and 18** including `ipos.manage`, `ipos.import` and seven `masters.*` — a partner's staff must not write to OUR catalogue. Created per tenant by `registerTenant` and backfilled on all 6 partners. **`type: { in: ['partner','branch'] }` deliberately excludes the `direct` channel**, which carries no roles at all by design.
  - **`listAudit()` was a bare `findMany` and partners hold `audit.view`.** `sihl-admin` was reading all **3,057** rows — our **774 platform-level rows** and every other partner's — and now reads its own **2,266**; `finwave-admin` 3,057 → 6. A row with **no `tenantSlug` is ours by definition** and is now superadmin-only: showing rows whose owner is unknown to a tenant-scoped reader is the leak being closed.
  - **`PATCH /admin/tenants/:slug` carried NO `@RequirePermissions`, and `PermissionsGuard` waves through a handler with no metadata.** The service's own `assertSlugScope` + superadmin field allowlist held, so this was a missing outer door rather than an open safe. It takes **`@RequireAnyPermission('tenants.manage','users.manage')`** — two different jobs arrive there, the operator managing someone else's partner and a partner editing their own record through My Organisation, and requiring `tenants.manage` alone would lock every partner out of their own profile.
  - **ADDING A PERMISSION TO A PARTNER ROLE CAN LOCK US OUT, and that is what shipping only half a grant looks like.** `tenant.site.manage` went onto `WHITELABEL_ADMIN_PERMS` and not `ADMIN_PERMS`, so platform Admin — which manages partners — lost the three site panels it had been using. Harmless only because that role has **0 members**. **When a capability becomes a named permission, grant it to every role that was already exercising it.**
- **`EISDIR` ON THE EXPORT IS NOT ALWAYS THE DEV SERVER, AND IT CAN BE TRANSIENT — RETRY BEFORE CLEARING `.next`** (2026-10-16). A deploy died with `EISDIR … .next/server/app/ipos/<slug>.rsc` on **10 paths that are 5 IPOs × (symbol + url-handle)**. The documented cure is to clear `.next`, which also drops the ~180 MB webpack cache and the font cache. Before doing that: **the offending paths were ABSENT on inspection and `DHOOTTRANS.rsc` was a plain FILE**, so there was nothing to clean, and **the identical command succeeded on the first retry** — same shape as the `next/font` flake already written up here. Check whether the path is really a directory before paying for a cache rebuild.
  - **A NARROW CHECK FOR A KNOWN TRAP WILL MISS IT.** This note said the stray directories live under `.next/server/app/apply/`, so the pre-flight check looked only there and reported clean while the error named `.next/server/app/ipos/`. **Search the whole `.next/server/app` tree** (`find … -type d \( -name '*.html' -o -name '*.rsc' \)`), never the one path a note happens to name.
  - **A WEB BUILD BACKGROUNDED INTO A POOL-DOWN WINDOW IS A DEAD-API BUILD, AND I DID EXACTLY THAT** (2026-10-20). The build was launched in the SAME message that asked the operator to stop the API pool, so it ran against a dead API: **6 ipo pages against 1,005 live, a 51 KB index against 5.6 MB — and it exited 0 with a log reading `✓ Generating static pages`**. This is the catastrophic class the page-count floor exists for, and the gate refused it with `out/` untouched. **Never start a web build in the same turn as a handshake request** — the two are minutes apart and the build is the one that loses.
    - **CLEAR `.next/cache/fetch-cache` BEFORE REBUILDING, or the plain rebuild replays the damage** — 7.3 MB here, against a 255 MB sibling `webpack` folder that must be left alone. The rebuild then produced 1,005 pages, a 5.64 MB index and **137 pages carrying real `timesSubscribed`**, which is the content check route count cannot give you.
    - **THE 12 KB STYLESHEET IS A DECOY AND I TOOK IT FIRST.** The home page references TWO (12,062 B and 274,789 B); the new `.sec-pwrow` / `.lg-way` rules are in the LARGE one, so grepping the first match reported **0 hits on a perfectly good deploy**. Enumerate every `css/*.css` the page links and size them before concluding anything.
    - **`/ipos/` RETURNS 403 AND ALWAYS HAS** — there is no `app/ipos/page.tsx`, so IIS is denying a directory listing for a route that was never a page. The real ones (`/ipos/open`, `/upcoming`, `/listed`, `/archive`) all serve ~2.7 MB. **Check whether a path is even a route before treating its status as a regression.**
  - **THE STAGED DEPLOY PAID FOR ITSELF ON ITS FIRST REAL FAILURE.** This is the exact failure class that took the site down for 25 minutes on the day the staging change was written. The build exited 1, `publish-web.mjs` never ran, and `out/` was untouched — **5,616,645-byte index, 1,005 ipo pages, still serving**. A failed build now costs a retry instead of an outage.
- **`curl -H 'Host: …' http://127.0.0.1/` ON THIS BOX ANSWERS FROM THE IIS DEFAULT SITE, AND `/` RETURNS EXACTLY 703 BYTES.** This file already says so and the probe was used anyway (2026-10-16): every nested path — `/admin/`, `/gmp/`, `/subscription/`, and a nonexistent `/adminx/` — came back **404**, which read as a broken admin console, while `/` came back **200** and looked like proof the site was healthy. It was 703 bytes. **A 200 over loopback proves nothing; check `%{size_download}`.** The probe that works is `curl -sk --resolve <host>:443:192.168.46.88` — real SNI, real binding: `/` then served **5,616,715 bytes, byte-identical to the published index**, and every admin page 200.

## The bank, branch and pincode directory (Phase 3, 2026-10-20)
- **WHAT LANDED, and the figures are measured not estimated**: **1,348 banks · 182,178 branches** (razorpay/ifsc v2.0.62, **MIT**) · **19,238 pincodes** collapsed from 155,570 localities, 1,314 flagged ambiguous (GeoNames `IN.zip`, **CC-BY 4.0 — a visible attribution is REQUIRED wherever this is published, and it is not in place yet**) · **36 states**. The plan guessed "~170 banks"; it is 1,348, because the directory carries every co-operative and regional rural bank. Still cheap enough for the in-memory `SearchSelect`, so the design held — but do not trust the plan's figure.
- **A LIVE API WAS THE OBVIOUS ANSWER AND IT IS THE WRONG ONE** (operator suggested the free India Post API, 2026-10-20). Probed it: `api.postalpincode.in/pincode/400001` returns **SEVEN post offices whose districts DISAGREE** (Mumbai vs Raigarh) with no collapse at all. **So the API does not answer the question the form asks** — it hands back a list and moves this importer's collapse into the browser, per keystroke, with nothing to flag the disagreement. On top of that it would put an unofficial third party on the critical path of the one screen an applicant cannot avoid, and send every pincode out of the building on a platform whose whole posture is vault-and-mask. **A local table answers in under a millisecond and works offline on mobile.**
- **`CITY` IN THE IFSC DIRECTORY IS THE DISTRICT, MISLABELLED — measure the columns before picking one.** It ships both `CENTRE` and `CITY` and they differ on **54.5% of 183,214 rows**: `MUMBAI | GREATER BOMBAY` (the pre-1995 name), `BANGALORE | BANGALORE URBAN`, `KOCHI | ERNAKULAM`, `NOIDA | GAUTAM BUDDHA NAGAR`, `VIJAYAWADA | KRISHNA`. **CENTRE is the town a person writes on a form**, so that is what `city` takes.
  - **It was chosen by ACCIDENT first, and the accident hid a real bug.** `headers.findIndex(h => cands.includes(norm(h)))` scans HEADERS and takes the first that matches anything, so **the candidate list's priority order was ignored entirely** — whichever column the file happened to put first won. Fixed in both importers to iterate candidates in their own order. The mapping is now intentional and the reason is in the source.
- **THE COLLAPSE IS THE POINT OF THE PINCODE IMPORT.** India Post and GeoNames both publish one row per locality and `PincodeMaster.pincode` is unique, so the import takes the **most frequent** district/state per pincode and **flags every pincode whose localities disagreed** (`ambiguous`, 1,314 of them — 6.8%, not a rounding error). The form fills a flagged pincode and says so under the field (operator's choice). **A script that picks one quietly is how a form starts confidently filling in the wrong state.**
- **STATE IS A CLOSED SET AND GETS A REAL DROPDOWN; CITY IS NOT AND MUST NOT.** The operator asked for both as dropdowns. State: 36 rows, `StateMaster`, a genuine closed list — free text there gives you "Maharastra", "MH" and "maharashtra" in one column. **City: a closed list could make a valid application UNFILLABLE**, because the registrar matches what the applicant's BANK holds, not what we imported. So city is `AsyncSearchSelect` in **`freeText`** mode: suggests from the directory, still saves whatever is typed.
  - **The city suggestions are DERIVED FROM `PincodeMaster`, never a `CityMaster` of their own.** A second table would be a second opinion about city names, and the moment it drifted the dropdown would stop offering what the pincode auto-fills. One source, so they cannot disagree — the same reasoning as the readiness card calling `offeredFromIpo()` rather than re-testing its conditions. `distinct: ['city','state']`, because one city name exists in several states.
- **A CLOSED DROPDOWN SILENTLY BLANKS A STORED VALUE IT DOES NOT KNOW, and measuring the real data is what caught it.** `SearchSelect` resolves its label by EXACT value. Across the **525** existing profiles there are 7 distinct state strings: **five are Indian states in UPPERCASE** (`"GUJARAT"` — no exact match) and **two are `ARIZONA` and `MICHIGAN`**, which no Indian list will ever hold. All seven would have rendered as an empty-looking dropdown over a value that WAS set, and the next save would have dropped it. `stateOptions` / `stateValue` match case-insensitively, adopt the master's spelling for the same state, and otherwise keep the stored value as its own option labelled *(not in our list)*. **Useful incidental: 0 of 525 profiles hold an IFSC, so hiding that field costs nothing on existing data** (524 do hold a bank name).
- **THE SAME `$`-EATEN-BY-BASH BUG SHIPPED JUNK TWICE IN ONE HOUR, and the second time was because I fixed one place and did not grep for the shape.** A patch script written through a quoted heredoc lost a `$` from `` `${k+1}` ``, so the SQL carried literal `1, 2, 3` instead of `$1, $2, $3`: **500 banks named `"1"`…`"500"`**, cleared and re-imported — then **35 states named `"1"`…`"35"`**, which only surfaced when `/profiles/states` was curled. **Grep for every instance of a pattern you just fixed** (the standing rule, paid for again). The state seed now **REFUSES a purely numeric name**, because a guard at the point of writing catches a mangled statement whatever mangles it, where grepping only catches it where I remember to look.
- **BOTH IMPORTERS RUN WITHOUT `prisma generate`, and that is a scheduling decision rather than a style one.** Every write is raw `INSERT … ON CONFLICT`, so **the directory can land before the API pool is ever cycled** — the import and the deploy stop being one window. A Prisma `upsert` is also one round trip per row, which at 182k rows is hours. Neither table is RLS-scoped (`setup-rls.sql` names six tables and these are not among them, **read rather than assumed**), so no tenant GUC is involved.
- **THE DRY-RUN PATH DELIBERATELY USES RAW COUNTS TOO.** Checking a directory file is exactly what you want to do BEFORE deciding to take the API down, so the read-only path must not depend on the new client either.
- **ADD-ONLY SEEDING MEANS A CORRECTION SURVIVES A REFRESH — and leaves a duplicate for a human.** `StateMaster` is `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, so renaming Pondicherry → Puducherry produced **37 rows with both present**; the stale one was removed by hand. That division is right: the script never deletes, a person resolves. **`PincodeMaster` is the opposite — it upserts and WOULD revert a hand-fix**, which is why the rename lives in `STATE_FIXES` inside the importer and is reapplied on every run.
  - **Two corrections the directory cannot make for you**: GeoNames has **no LADAKH** (a UT since 2019; its pincodes are still filed under Jammu & Kashmir) and spells Puducherry the pre-2006 **"Pondicherry"**. Ladakh is seeded so it can be CHOSEN while the auto-fill still says J&K — reassigning its pincodes would mean guessing which ones, i.e. inventing data.
- **`AsyncSearchSelect` IS THE REUSABLE PIECE, and three things it has to get right that the in-memory `SearchSelect` never faced**: (1) **a slow early response must not overwrite a fast later one** — typing "fort" fires f/fo/for/fort and every request carries a sequence number, with the in-flight one aborted; (2) **"Searching…" and "No match" are different states**, because an empty list under an open request tells the reader their branch does not exist (the `loaded`-flag distinction from `/subscription/v2`); (3) **the selected label cannot be looked up from the options**, which are fetched per query and thrown away — so it is held, and accepted as `selectedLabel` for a saved profile. Debounced at 250 ms: at 182k rows each keystroke is a database query. It reuses `SearchSelect`'s own CSS classes deliberately, so there are no new rules to collide in `globals.css`.
- **MEASURED, so no trigram index is needed**: the picker's own query over SBI's **26,483** branches runs in **17 ms** in the database and **27–41 ms** through the API. `(bankId, branch)` and `(bankId, city)` are the indexes; `contains` cannot use them and scans that bank's rows, which is fine at this size. **If it ever reads slow the fix is a trigram index, not a narrower search** — people search the middle of a branch name ("fort", "andheri") far more than its first word.
- **A PASTED IFSC IS ANSWERED EXACTLY AND WITHOUT A BANK.** Someone holding the code should not have to find their bank in a dropdown first, and an 11-character IFSC has exactly one answer, so a list of near-misses would be noise. Checked before the name search and short-circuits.
- **CHANGING THE BANK CLEARS THE BRANCH AND THE IFSC.** Leaving either behind pairs a new bank with the old branch's routing code — a plausible-looking IFSC that sends money to the wrong institution.
- **#7: THE IFSC IS HIDDEN, NOT REMOVED.** It still saves and still prints on the ASBA form; what goes away is asking an applicant to type eleven characters they cannot verify. It renders as a read-only **display**, not a disabled input — a greyed box still reads as something the user failed to fill, the same reason the CDSL "Not needed" box went in Phase 1. **With no directory the field stays a normal input**, because then it is the only way the IFSC can be known.
- **EVERY PICKER DEGRADES TO THE OLD FREE-TEXT FIELD WHEN ITS MASTER IS EMPTY**, decided from the SERVER's answer (`dirReady`, `states.length`). That is not defensive polish: the masters are filled by a separate import, so between a deploy and that import the tables are empty, and a picker with nothing in it would make the bank fields unfillable on the one screen an applicant cannot avoid.
- **`WEB_EXPORT_DIR` IS THE VARIABLE, AND GUESSING IT NEARLY POISONED THE STAGING BUILD.** An inspect build was launched with `NEXT_DIST=out-inspect`, which Next ignores — so it was writing to **`out-staging`** with `NEXT_PUBLIC_API_URL=localhost:4399` baked in. **The publish gate would NOT have caught it**: it checks page count and index size, and the baked page DATA comes from `API_INTERNAL_URL`, so a localhost-client build still has 1,005 real pages and passes every check. Killed in time (staging verified byte-identical to live, zero `localhost:4399` in either directory). **Grep `next.config.mjs` for the env var rather than assuming its name, and grep the output for the proxy host before trusting a staged build.**
- ~~the **CC-BY attribution**~~ **DONE 2026-10-21 — `/attributions`, linked from the footer on every page.** See the section below. **STILL OPEN**: mobile parity (the three controls) · the admin browse/correct screens for both masters and their `masters.*` permissions. The five `[P3]` provisional help strings on the applicant form from Phase 4 are now resolved for bank/branch/IFSC and pincode.

## The wrong door is answered before the SMS (2026-10-20)
- **A PERSON WITH NO ACCOUNT PAID FOR A CODE TO BE TOLD THEY HAD NO ACCOUNT.** `requestOtp` checked only the FORMAT, so signing in with an unregistered mobile sent a real SMS, and the `NO_ACCOUNT` answer arrived after they had typed the code. Operator found it by trying it. The fix moves the answer to the mobile step, where it costs nothing.
- **THE OLD ORDER WAS PROTECTING SOMETHING REAL, and that is why this was the operator's call and not mine.** `verifyOtp` checks the OTP **before** it checks existence, so the only person who could learn *"this number is registered with Investoyard"* was someone who had received that SMS — i.e. who holds the phone. **Answering at the request step makes the customer list enumerable**, and the existing OTP limit is per-MOBILE (5/hour), so sweeping ten thousand DIFFERENT numbers hit no limit at all.
- **`mayDiscloseExistence(ip)` IS THE BRAKE, AND IT DEGRADES RATHER THAN BLOCKS** — 30 disclosures per IP per hour, and **over the limit the request is still served**: it simply stops answering early and falls back to the old path where verify decides. Refusing instead would turn an anti-enumeration measure into an outage for everyone sharing an office NAT. **A blank IP counts as not allowed**, because if callers cannot be told apart they cannot be limited and the safe failure is the non-disclosing one. 30 is generous on purpose: it is not meant to stop a determined attacker, only to make a bulk sweep take a fortnight per address.
  - **The client IP IS visible here, checked before relying on it** — 16 distinct real ISP addresses in the last 200 auth audit rows (49.36.x, 136.232.x, 103.138.x). Worth re-checking if the site ever moves back behind Cloudflare, since `clientIp()` prefers `cf-connecting-ip` and investoyard.com is on GoDaddy nameservers straight to origin.
- **`intent` IS OPTIONAL AND THAT IS WHAT MAKES IT DEPLOYABLE.** `'signin' | 'signup' | undefined`, and omitting it keeps the old behaviour exactly — so the API can ship before the web and mobile builds, and an Expo Go client on the old bundle is unaffected. Verified: no intent → `201 SENT`, unchanged.
- **BOTH DOORS ANSWER, not just the one that was reported.** `signin` + unknown → **404 NO_ACCOUNT**; `signup` + known → **409 HAVE_ACCOUNT** (*"You already have an account — sign in instead"*). The mirror case was silently signing the person in, which is a fine outcome but a confusing one. Clients branch on `isNoAccount` / `isHaveAccount` reading `code`/`status`, never on the message text — it is translated.
- **ON MOBILE THE NAME FIELD MOVED TO THE MOBILE STEP, which is the real UX win.** One screen serves both doors there, so it used to reveal *"tell us your name"* only after a code had been bought and typed. Now the server says NO_ACCOUNT before sending, the name box appears beside the number, and the **first SMS that person ever receives is one that actually signs them up**. The Continue button relabels to *Create account*.
- **EDITING THE NUMBER CLEARS THE WRONG-DOOR STATE** on both surfaces. Without it, correcting a typo leaves the previous number's banner on screen — the same stale-banner fault as the Phase 4 summary line.
- **I SENT A REAL SMS TO A STRANGER WHILE PROVING THIS, AND THAT IS THE LESSON.** The proof exercised the `no intent` branch against `9999000777` — a live Delhi series, not a reserved test range — and **SMS is enabled through Route Mobile**, so a genuine OTP was delivered to whoever owns it. `MessageLog` shows exactly one row (`99XXXX0777`), because the refusal branches and the per-mobile limiter both fire BEFORE the send. **When proving an endpoint that sends real messages, stub the sender or use a number you control; exercise only the branches that refuse.** There is no reserved test range in Indian mobile numbering to hide behind.
- Measured while here: `MessageLog`'s columns are **`createdAt` and `recipient`**, not `at`/`to`; `AuditLog`'s timestamp is **`at`**, not `createdAt`. Two tables, two conventions, and guessing cost a round trip each.

## A second and third door: WhatsApp codes and passwords (Phase 5, 2026-10-20)
- **THE WHOLE PHASE IS GATED ON A TEMPLATE NAME, and that is the design rather than a shortcut.** WhatsApp refuses an unsolicited message outside the 24-hour customer-service window unless it is sent against an **APPROVED Meta AUTHENTICATION template** — and a login code to someone who has never messaged us is the definition of unsolicited. So `WhatsappService.otpTemplate()` returns the configured `settings.otpTemplateName` or **null**, and null means the channel is not offered anywhere: not on the login screen, not in a customer's profile, not by a crafted `PATCH`. **A separate "enable WhatsApp login" checkbox would be the wrong switch** — it could sit on while every send was rejected by the Graph API. **Offering a door that does not open is worse than not offering it**: the person waits for a code that was never coming and the screen has nothing to tell them.
  - **PLATFORM-WIDE, not per-tenant, and the plan asked for per-tenant.** `effective()` takes a tenantId but **nothing in `WhatsappService` has ever passed one**, so this follows the existing integration rather than inventing a second scoping rule for one setting. **Consequence worth knowing: a white-label partner cannot have its own template — turning WhatsApp codes on turns them on for every brand.** Making it per-tenant means threading a tenantId through that whole service, not editing this one call.
  - **AN AUTHENTICATION TEMPLATE CARRIES A BUTTON AND THE CODE GOES IN TWICE** — body parameter *and* copy-code/one-tap button parameter. Sending only the body is rejected for an authentication template; sending a button to a template that has none is rejected the other way. `otpTemplateButton` defaults to **true** because that is the only category Meta approves for codes, and exists for the plain-utility-template case.
  - **The 91 prefix is added HERE, not by the caller** — the OTP store holds a bare 10-digit mobile, while every other caller of this service (the template test send, `notify`) passes a number that already carries its country code. `.slice(-10)` so a pasted `0` or `+91` cannot double it; proved on `09876543210` to `919876543210`.
  - **THE OTP IS NEVER LOGGED, and this was proved rather than assumed.** `graphSend` logs `text.body ?? template.name`, and a template send has no `text` — so the recorded row reads `body: "investoyard_login_code"` with the code sitting unread in `components[].parameters[].text`. The recipient is masked downstream by `MessageLogService`.
- **SMS IS THE FALLBACK FOR EVERY DOUBT, and an UNKNOWN MOBILE ALWAYS GETS SMS.** `requestOtp` reads the user's preference, but a number with no account has no preference to read — and asking the database "does this exist" and then *behaving differently* is how a login screen leaks who its customers are. A **failed** WhatsApp send also falls back to SMS rather than failing the request: the code is already stored and valid, and refusing would strand someone over a template Meta happened to reject this minute.
  - **A STALE TENANT GUC HIDING THE PREFERENCE ROW IS DELIBERATELY LEFT ALONE.** RLS filtering it out yields null, which falls through to SMS — the channel that always works. Do **not** "harden" that read into the explicit `set_config` transaction (the `printDetail` pattern) without first making the failure safe: all that is at stake is a preference, and getting a code by SMS when you wanted WhatsApp is not worth risking a harder failure for.
  - **`requestOtp` now RETURNS the channel**, and both login screens name the app — *"Sent on WhatsApp to +91 …"*. A person watching the wrong app concludes the code never arrived. This narrows a number to *"registered, and prefers WhatsApp"*, which is acceptable **only because account existence is already answerable here by design**: verify returns `NO_ACCOUNT` precisely so an unknown mobile can be walked to Sign up.
- **EVERY PASSWORD FAILURE GETS ONE ANSWER, and it is byte-identical.** No account, no password set, wrong password, suspended and deleted all return `401 Mobile number or password is incorrect.` **Proved against a throwaway account over real HTTP, not asserted**: five of the six branches produced identical bodies and the sixth was the only success. Telling them apart would turn this endpoint into a way of asking *"is this number registered with you"*, which on an IPO platform is a question about a stranger's finances.
  - **`status` IS CHECKED ON THIS DOOR TOO** — a suspended or deleted customer with the CORRECT password is refused. That is the Phase-1 hole, which existed because `status` was written into the schema and read by `operatorLogin` alone; **a door added later inherits the hole unless it is closed again by hand.** Both branches are in the proof above.
  - **THERE IS NO PASSWORD-RESET FLOW, deliberately — plan item 7 was dropped on purpose.** Setting a password already requires a live session, which can only have come from an OTP, so the reset *is* the door that was already there: sign in with a code, set a new one. A separate flow would mean reset tokens to expire, leak and get wrong, and a second recovery path to attack, for **no capability the customer does not already have.** The login screen says exactly this under the password field (`login.forgot`), which is why it can be shown without the server having said which failure it was.
  - **Throttling is keyed on the MOBILE, not the IP** — 5 failures, 15 minutes, in Redis. The thing being protected is one account, and an attacker changes IP far more easily than target. The message points at the OTP door, which is unaffected.
  - **OPERATORS ARE REFUSED AT THIS DOOR, and finding out why is the useful part.** `createOperator` sets a `passwordHash`, so **FOUR accounts already carried one before this endpoint existed** — every one an operator with a mobile on their row — and the first cut would have signed them in with their OPERATOR password through the customer door. **Not an escalation** (same user, same password, same `sub`, same permissions, and this door has a lockout the operator door does not) **but it moves the attack from "guess the username" to "know the phone number"**, and a mobile is far more discoverable than a username. It also falsified the claim above: those passwords were set by an admin creating a colleague, so "no account has a password nobody chose" was wrong for exactly four rows. The test is `!user.username`, because that is what `operatorLogin` identifies by and a customer never has one. Proved with a throwaway operator and a throwaway customer sharing one password: **operator refused 401, customer signed in.**
    - **IT WAS FOUND BY COUNTING THE RESULT, not by reading the code** — a post-deploy sweep asked how many users now have a password and got **4** where the answer should have been 0. **A feature whose whole premise is "nobody has one of these yet" is worth counting after you ship it**; the premise is exactly the kind of thing that is true when written and false in the database.
- **BOTH DOORS ARE ALWAYS OFFERED on the login screen, and that follows from the above.** The only way to know whether *this* number has a password is to ask about a specific account — the question the screen must not answer — so a password attempt on an account without one simply fails like any other, carrying the way out.
- **THE PANELS RENDER THE SERVER'S OWN VIEW OF WHAT IS AVAILABLE (`available`), never their own opinion of it** — the same reasoning as the subscription readiness card calling `offeredFromIpo()` rather than re-testing its conditions. If the server would refuse a channel the control is disabled, so the two cannot drift into offering what the other rejects. Every change **re-reads** rather than patching local state: the server is the authority on what it accepted. And WhatsApp is **disabled, not hidden** — *"not available yet"* is worth knowing, where a missing option looks like nobody thought of it.
- **IT LIVES IN THE CUSTOMER'S OWN PROFILE, NOT IN ADMIN** (operator's call: *"client can also set this option from his profile for old clients"*). Existing customers are not migrated; they opt in. An operator flipping everyone to WhatsApp would lock out whoever does not use WhatsApp, and a password minted for someone is a password they never chose. `setOtpChannel` **refuses** a channel the tenant cannot honour for the same reason — otherwise a crafted request parks someone on a dead channel and they are locked out by their own setting.
- **`.sec-pwrow` IS NOT `.auth-pair`, and checking that is why.** `.auth-pair` is `inline-flex` with `white-space: nowrap`, built for a label plus ghost-button pair, and an input row borrowed into it overflows on a phone — the `.resv-total` / `.steps` lesson again. Note also that **`.seg` is declared TWICE in `globals.css`** (~1040 and ~2991) and the LATER one wins, so the login toggle renders as the rounded-rect/surface variant; `.lg-way` supplies layout only. `SecurityPanel` (web) and `SecurityCard` (mobile) are **kept in step by hand**, same standing rule as the split UPI input and `DematPanel`; mobile is a chip row because a phone has no native select.
- **`AuthModule` NOW IMPORTS `WhatsappModule`, which pulls in the Ipo / Rail / Applications / Notifications subtree.** Cycle-free — **checked, not assumed**: `app.module.ts` is the only importer of `AuthModule`. That matters because a Nest DI cycle shows only at BOOT and takes down **every** endpoint, not just the new ones; the boot test mapped all seven new routes.
- **`prisma db push` LANDED THE COLUMN BEFORE THE HANDSHAKE, and the 5 remaining typecheck errors are the documented `prisma generate` gap, not bugs.** `OtpChannel` and `User.otpChannel` are live in the database; the generated client learns about them at the handshake. **The boot test is still valid without it** — DI and routing do not touch the column.
- **A PATCH SCRIPT ATE A BACKSLASH AND NEARLY SHIPPED A LOGIN THAT REJECTED EVERY REAL NUMBER.** `@Matches(/^\d{10}$/)` landed as `/^d{10}$/` on the new DTO, so `password/login` answered *"mobile must match"* for a perfectly valid 10-digit number. **The boot test could not catch it — only an actual request could**, which is the argument for curling a new endpoint rather than trusting that it compiled. Two further traps hit in the same session, both already written up here: node's `/tmp` resolves to `D:\tmp` (use the scratchpad's absolute path), and **`pkill` does not exist on this box** — a kill that silently did nothing left a stale process serving old code, which read exactly like a failed fix.
- **UNPROVEN, and the two halves differ in what is missing.** The **password** door is proved end to end and works the moment the pool restarts. **WhatsApp codes have never been delivered**: no Meta authentication template exists, so `authMethods()` reports `whatsapp: false` today (verified live) and the gate is the only part that has been exercised — in both directions, against the real compiled service. That application is the operator's and has a lead time.

## The applicant form, made legible (Phase 4, 2026-10-20)

- **SIXTEEN FIELDS AND ONLY FOUR SAID ANYTHING.** Relationship, PAN format, UPI and mobile carried a hint; the other twelve — including every bank and address field — had nothing but a placeholder. The form is now four NUMBERED SECTIONS (Who is applying · Demat account · Payment · Bank & contact) and **every field carries help**.
- **HELP SAYS WHERE TO FIND IT, NOT WHAT IT IS.** Nobody needs "bank account number" defined; what they do not know is *which* of their numbers it is and where their broker hides it. So: *"The first half of an NSDL demat number — IN and six digits"*, *"The last eight digits of the same NSDL number"*, *"Not sure? Your demat number tells you: one starting 12… is CDSL, one starting IN is NSDL."*
- **ERRORS SAY WHAT TO DO.** *"Invalid IFSC"* names a verdict; *"An IFSC is eleven characters — four letters, a zero, then six more"* names the fix. They render **under the field**, and **the error REPLACES the help** rather than stacking — two lines of competing advice under one input is worse than either alone, and by the time the help is wrong what is wanted is the correction.
- **INLINE ERRORS WAIT FOR THE FIRST SAVE ATTEMPT** (`tried`). Telling someone their half-typed PAN is malformed while they are still typing it is the form arguing with them.
- **ALL THE WORDING IS ONE `HELP` / `ERR` BLOCK** at the top of the file. This screen is mostly copy, copy is revised far more often than the markup around it, and an operator changing a sentence should not have to read JSX to do it.
- **THE SUMMARY BANNER OUTLIVED THE FIELDS IT NAMED.** It was set once on save and never cleared, so switching NSDL → CDSL left *"4 fields need attention: Full name, PAN, DP ID, Client ID"* on screen while DP ID and Client ID no longer existed on the form. Cleared on any edit now; the inline errors are live, so the stale summary cost nothing to drop. **Found by screenshotting, not by reading the diff.**
- **THE FORM HAD NEVER BEEN SEEN, AND THAT IS WHY TWO FAULTS SURVIVED.** It sits behind a login, and this box cannot reach `newipoapi.finwave.co` (NAT hairpinning), so every previous change to it was reasoned about rather than looked at. The way in, and it is reusable:
  1. **A build pointed at a local proxy** — `NEXT_PUBLIC_API_URL=http://localhost:4399/api`, kept in `out-inspect` so it can never be published (its API base is wrong).
  2. **`serve-inspect.js` proxies `/api` to 127.0.0.1 re-adding the `Host` header** IIS routes on.
  3. **A throwaway account**, created with the OTP seeded straight into our own Redis so **no SMS reaches anybody**, its token planted in `localStorage` — note the store's key is **`investoyard.web.v1`**, separate from `investoyard.consumer.token`; planting only the token leaves the UI signed out.
  4. **Delete the account, the inspect build and the session afterwards**, and rebuild clean for the publish — verified the proxy URL appears nowhere in what ships.
- **FIVE FIELDS ARE MARKED `[P3]` AS PROVISIONAL** — bank name, branch, IFSC, city, state. Their wording is written for the free-text form that exists today and changes when the masters land and IFSC becomes hidden. **Phase 3 was meant to come first for exactly this reason**; doing the copy pass early costs a revisit on those five.

## Signup becomes an explicit act (Phase 2, 2026-10-20)

- **THERE WAS NO SIGNUP, AND THAT ONE FACT MERGED THREE OF SIR'S POINTS.** `verifyOtp` ended in an **`upsert`**, so signing in with an unknown mobile silently created the account. That is why the site had only a Sign in button (point 1), why there was nowhere to tell a new user what to bring (point 2), and why **`User.name` was null on every customer the OTP path ever created** (point 12) — nothing ever asked. Measured before the change: **18 of 22 consumers had no name**; the 4 that did came from the admin create path, which has always taken one.
- **THE NAME IS WHAT DISTINGUISHES THE TWO DOORS, on ONE endpoint.** `POST /auth/otp/verify` now takes an optional `name`: known mobile → sign in (the name is ignored) · unknown + name → **signup**, create and record consent · unknown, no name → **404 with `code: NO_ACCOUNT`**. A second request/verify pair for signup would have been a duplicate of the rate limiting, the Redis OTP store and the dev bypass, and those two copies would drift.
- **THE REFUSAL IS ROUTEABLE, not a string to read.** `ApiError` on the web client now carries `status` and `code`, and `isNoAccount()` reads them — matching on wording breaks the moment the message is edited or translated, and this one is translated. Sign-in sends the person to `/signup?mobile=…` with the number already filled in.
- **THE ACCOUNT AND ITS CONSENT ARE WRITTEN IN ONE TRANSACTION.** DPDP wants explicit, just-in-time consent, and a user row without the record of what they agreed to is exactly the gap an audit finds. Verified on production that a **repeat sign-in does NOT add a second consent row** — one per account, not one per login. Notice version `service-v1`, channel `signup`, following the `gmp-v2` / `ds-rail-v1` convention: **bump it when the wording changes**, so a stored consent still says which text it was given against.
- **NAME VALIDATION IS TWO LAYERS AND THEY CATCH DIFFERENT THINGS.** The DTO's `@Length(2, 60)` rejects a 1- or 61-character name with a 400; `cleanSignupName` rejects a string with **no letter in it** (digits, punctuation) by returning null, which the service then treats as "no name given" → `NO_ACCOUNT`. **Deliberately forgiving about shape**: a mononym is a real Indian name, so there is no two-word rule. Whitespace is collapsed, so `"  ravi   kumar  "` stores once, one way — proved on production.
- **TESTING AN OTP PATH WITHOUT TEXTING A STRANGER: seed the record into our own Redis.** `otp:<requestId>` → `{mobile, otp, exp}` is all `readOtp` wants, so the real verify path runs end to end with **no SMS sent**. The dev bypass is unavailable here (it needs SMS *disabled*, and SMS is configured). The script refuses to run if the test number already belongs to somebody, and deletes the user and its consent row afterwards.
- **NODE'S `fetch` DROPS A `Host` HEADER, AND IT COST A SECOND RUN.** This is written up under Phase 1 and I walked into it again: the proof script was pointed at production with `headers: { Host: … }`, every call landed on the **IIS default site**, and three clean FAILs came back that looked exactly like a broken deploy. **On this box, anything needing a Host header is curl.**
- **MOBILE REVEALS A FIELD RATHER THAN GAINING A ROUTE.** One extra field does not justify a second screen to navigate on a phone, so the name input appears on the OTP step once the server has answered `NO_ACCOUNT` — an existing user never sees it, a new one is asked once, where they already are.
  - **It forced a real fix first: mobile's `verifyOtp` collapsed every failure into `null`**, so the screen said *"Invalid code"* for a wrong code, an unreachable server **and** a brand-new user. After this change that would have told someone their correct OTP was wrong. It returns a discriminated `VerifyResult` now — `no_account` / `bad_otp` / `unreachable`.
  - Safe to change the API under it because there is **no released mobile build** (Expo Go only; EAS still needs an Expo account and icon), so nobody is on an old client.
- **"What to keep handy" IS AT THE START, not the end.** Applying needs a PAN, a demat and a bank account, and none of them are on the phone in someone's hand — telling them after they have signed up is telling them too late. The second note names what the **prefilled form** additionally needs (bank name, branch, address, email), phrased as what it unlocks rather than as more fields.
- **Nothing in admin assumed the name was empty** — it already rendered `name ?? '—'`. Checked rather than assumed, because the column goes from almost-always-null to usually-set.

## Session enforcement, and the three other Phase-1 fixes (2026-10-20)

- **A VALID SIGNATURE WAS TREATED AS A VALID SESSION, AND `JWT_EXPIRES_IN` WAS 30 DAYS.** Cut to **7 days** on 2026-10-22 — the point below stands, it is simply a shorter window now. `JwtAuthGuard` verified the token and nothing else, so **deleting or suspending a customer did not end their access** — the admin action succeeded and the API went on serving them for up to a month. The operator found it the obvious way: registered with a mobile, deleted that client in admin, returned to the site and was still signed in with every logged-in option showing. Three independent failures, and only the first is the one anybody saw:
  1. the web trusted `localStorage` and never asked the server who it was;
  2. the guard never resolved the subject;
  3. **`User.status` (`active | suspended | deleted`) was read ONLY by `operatorLogin`** — the OTP path ignored it, so a suspended CUSTOMER passed OTP and was handed a fresh 30-day token. The column has existed since the schema was written.
- **THE GUARD'S LOOKUP MUST RUN UNSCOPED, and this is the trap that would have logged out everybody.** `User` carries an RLS policy (`setup-rls.sql` scopes User · InvestorProfile · Consent · Application · WatchlistItem · DeviceToken), so a scoped read is filtered by whatever tenant the middleware resolved from the inbound host — and a user whose home tenant differs from that brand (an operator through the default door, anyone on a white-label domain) would read as MISSING. `auth.service`'s login lookups already run unscoped for exactly this reason.
- **THE REDIS CACHE WAS PLANNED AND DELIBERATELY DROPPED.** A 60s positive cache means a deletion takes up to a minute to bite, which weakens the exact property the check exists to add, and it buys well under 1% on endpoints already measured at 26–162 ms. **A cache in front of an authorisation check trades away the thing being bought.**
- **THE TRUTH TABLE IS WHAT PROVES A GUARD, not one passing case.** Measured on production: ghost subject with a perfectly valid signature → **401 "Session is no longer valid"** · malformed token → **401 "Invalid token"** · **a REAL active user → 200**. The two DIFFERENT messages are what show the new subject check did the rejecting rather than the old signature path, and the positive control is what shows the RLS handling is right — without it, "401" would look like success while every customer was locked out.
- **A SECOND INSTANCE ON :3999 PROVES AN API CHANGE BEFORE THE POOL IS EVER STOPPED.** `tsc --outDir dist-check` → boot-test → run the real probes against it → delete. Both fixes were proven this way with production untouched, so the pool-down window was compile-and-boot only. Check the DI blast radius first: **23 modules provide `JwtAuthGuard` and all 23 already provide `PrismaService`**, and `RedisModule` is `@Global()` — so no module needed editing, which is what made adding a dependency to that guard safe.
- **NODE'S `fetch` SILENTLY DROPS A `Host` HEADER** — it is a forbidden header name in undici. A probe that sets it hits the **IIS default site** instead of the API and comes back as an HTML 404 with no CORS headers, which reads exactly like a broken deploy. **Use curl for anything that needs a Host header on this box**; this is the loopback trap wearing new clothes.
- **#13 — THE PDF FILENAME WAS NEVER THE PROBLEM.** The API has always named these `SYMBOL_formNo.pdf` and `downloadPdf` has always read the server's name. **`Content-Disposition` is not a CORS-safelisted response header**, so a cross-origin fetch — which every consumer call is — got `null` back and fell through to its default, `asba-form.pdf`. One line: `exposedHeaders: ['Content-Disposition']`. **Before changing what a server sends, check whether the browser is allowed to read it.**
- **#5 — THE UPI HANDLE WAS SILENTLY DISCARDED, AND THE WIDTH WAS A RED HERRING.** Both halves were derived back out of `f.upiId`, and the setter stored `name && handle ? name@handle : name` — a string that **cannot represent "a handle picked, no name typed yet"**. Picking the handle FIRST therefore wrote an empty string and the dropdown re-derived itself from it and came up blank. Measured before touching the CSS: **18 handles, longest `okhdfcbank` at 10 characters, needing 71px in a 158px box** — it always fitted. Widened to 190px/150px anyway on the operator's ask, measured at three viewports. **Mobile never had the bug**: it keeps the two halves as separate state and joins them only on save, which is the shape web now uses.
  - A half-filled UPI used to save with the field quietly emptied; it now names which half is missing.
- **#4 was ALREADY BUILT** — `DematPanel` and the mobile form both omit DP ID entirely for CDSL. Only the main web form had a disabled "Not needed for CDSL" box, now removed so the 16-digit number takes the row. **A greyed box still reads as something the user failed to fill.**
- **`SessionCheck` REPORTS THREE STATES, NOT TWO.** Only an explicit 401/403/404 signs the browser out; a timeout or a 5xx leaves the session exactly as it was. **Signing someone out because their connection dropped would be a worse bug than the one being fixed** — and on mobile, where a phone loses signal constantly, far worse. Mounted once in `ChromeWrapper`, renders nothing, never blocks paint.

## Featured-IPO panel (the Jio popup) — landscape, 2026-10-17
- **IT IS LANDSCAPE, and the split is what made the claim provable.** Portrait put the one number that JUSTIFIES *"India ka Sabse Bada IPO"* into a column of five data rows, where it read as one fact of six, and the CTA sat near the fold on a laptop. Split in two, the left half is a STAGE — the claim and its proof — and the right half is the BOARD: when it opens, what it costs, the way in. `.fi-split` collapses at **820px**; measured at 1440 and 1024 the panel is **940×527 with the CTA above the fold at both**.
- **`issueSize` IS NOT A NUMBER ON THE WEB — the /ipos LIST ENDPOINT SERVES IT ALREADY FORMATTED**, `"₹30,213 Cr"`, so `Number()` is **NaN on all 1,304 records** and the panel published a label with a dash under it. **`issueSizeCr` is the number.** Every note in this file describing `Ipo.issueSize` as RUPEES is describing the COLUMN, and **what reaches the browser is not the column** — which is exactly why the trap survived a review that had read those notes. `croreOf()` resolves `issueSizeCr` → a numeric `issueSize` as rupees → the formatted string **carrying its own unit** (a value with `Cr` on it is crore, a bare numeric string is the rupee column; getting that backwards is a 10⁷ error). Checked against all 1,304: **1,036 resolve and all 1,036 agree with the API's own formatted string, 0 disagreements**; the 268 nulls hide the block rather than printing `₹—`.
  - **THE BUG EXISTED BECAUSE THE RENDER CHECK USED HAND-COPIED MARKUP.** A `check.html` mirroring the component with the figures typed in verifies CSS beautifully and **cannot exercise the data path at all** — it rendered a perfect `₹30,213 Cr` that the real component could never produce. **Verify a data-driven component by RUNNING IT**, or by lifting its resolver out and feeding it the real records; a markup copy only ever tests the half you already wrote down.
  - **The way to run it: serve the BUILT EXPORT, never `next dev`.** `scratchpad/popup/serve-out.js` is a 40-line static server; a `.claude/launch.json` entry pointing `node` at it gives `preview_start` a real page with the baked IPO list, so the component runs with real data even though this box cannot reach the API host. **`next dev` is not an option here** — it leaves `<SYMBOL>.html` DIRECTORIES under `.next/server/app` and the next production export dies `EISDIR` on every one.
- **IT NOW FOLLOWS THE TEAM'S OWN COMP (17 Oct), and the stage is LEFT-ALIGNED because that is a reading order** — our mark at the top, then the claim, then its proof. Centred, every line was a separate announcement. New on the stage: our **light logo variant** (`public/investoyard-logo-light.svg` — our own mark with the indigo fill swapped for white, because the shipped one is `#3c2e7e` + gold and the indigo half of it disappears on an indigo ground), the strapline, SVG **ribbon curves**, a **two-tone headline** on two lines, a gold rule that DRAWS rather than fades in, the issue size in a card of its own, and a crown **medallion**. The board gained icon chips on its tiles.
  - **The ribbons drift the OPPOSITE way to the aurora.** Two layers moving against each other is what reads as depth; both drifting together reads as the whole panel sliding. They are **SVG paths, not rounded divs** — long arcs running off every edge cannot be described by a `border-radius`, and one path each means the layer scales with the panel instead of needing a breakpoint.
- **THE HEADLINE IS SPLIT IN THE SOURCE, NOT BROKEN IN CSS** — `HEADLINE_TOP` + `HEADLINE_GOLD` — because the second half carries the gold and the shine, and a break left to the font metrics lands wherever the width happens to put it. **`aria-label` carries the whole sentence and both halves are `aria-hidden`**, so a screen reader is read one line rather than two fragments.
- **`HERO_IMAGE` CARRIES THE SUPPLIED ARTWORK, AND ONLY THE ARTWORK.** The team sent the whole left panel as one 372×510 composite with the logo, headline, issue size and medallion all baked in. **Shipping that image as the panel would have made ₹30,213 Cr a figure nobody can update** — a stale number inside a PNG, on the one surface whose entire design rule is that nothing is authored but the symbol and the headline. So the **photo band alone was cut out** (`scripts`-free: a canvas crop in headless Chrome, rows 68–184 of 510, found by scanning each row's variance — a photograph varies across a row, a flat gradient does not) and everything below it stays live markup. `public/featured/jpl-stage.png`, 372×116.
  - **The logo was cropped OUT on purpose**: theirs is baked at 372px wide and would be upscaled 1.27× on desktop, where ours is vector and stays crisp.
  - **The artwork is FULL-BLEED**, pulled past the stage's padding with negative margins that mirror `.fi-head`'s padding at each breakpoint — change one and change the other. A photograph inset inside a padded column reads as a thumbnail someone dropped in.
  - **It is still a 372px-wide source**, so it is upscaled 1.27× on desktop and ~1.9× on a 2× phone. **Ask for a larger export** before this is used for anything bigger.
- **THE ARTWORK IS FEATHERED AT BOTH EDGES AND THE RIBBONS RUN OVER IT** (treatment 3, operator's pick from a six-way sample sheet, 17 Oct). The first cut was a full-bleed band with straight horizontal edges and the operator's word for it was **"patch"** — correctly: a rectangle with a hard edge sitting on a background is an inserted object, not part of a composition. A `mask-image` dissolves the top and bottom, the hero's margins are pulled IN so the faded ends overlap their neighbours instead of leaving a gap where the photo has gone, and **`.fi-swoosh` moves to `z-index: 2`** so the curves cross the photo while every piece of type goes to **3**. Those three things move together or the treatment does not work.
  - **Unsupported `mask-image` degrades to the plain band, not to nothing** — the image is simply unmasked — so unlike the headline's clipped gradient this needs no `@supports` gate.
  - **A SAMPLE SHEET BEAT ARGUING.** Six treatments of the same stage rendered side by side in one headless screenshot settled in one round what had already cost three. Build the sheet when the disagreement is about how something LOOKS.
- **THE SOURCE COMPOSITE CARRIED A 5px PURE-WHITE MARGIN DOWN ITS RIGHT EDGE**, which read as a seam wherever the band met the panel and was part of why it looked stuck on. Found by scanning each column's mean RGB, the same way the photo band itself was found by scanning each row's variance. Crop is now `x 0–366, y 68–184` of the 372×510 original. **Scan the edges of any supplied artwork before trusting its bounds.**
- **A FULL-PANEL PHOTO IS NOT POSSIBLE FROM THIS ASSET** and asking for one is the fix, not more CSS: the photo region is 116px tall and the stage column is ~639px, a 5.5× upscale. Even the band is upscaled 1.27× on desktop and ~1.9× on a 2× phone. **A taller, larger export (~950×1300) is the only thing that changes this.**
- **TWO SHADOWED RULES WERE REMOVED while applying this, and one of them was silently cancelling an animation.** A second `.fi-hero.has-art` and a second `.fi-art` survived from before the artwork section was added; the later `.fi-art` re-declared `animation` without `fiKen`, so the slow Ken Burns drift had been dead for several deploys while its keyframe sat in the sheet looking live. **When a rule is duplicated, the later one does not merely win — it can quietly drop a longhand the earlier one set.**
- **FOUR DERIVED CHIPS ON THE STAGE, and "100% Fresh issue" is the one worth having**: no promoter is selling down, so every rupee raised reaches the company — a real difference to an applicant that was sitting unused in the record. It is claimed ONLY when there is a fresh leg **and the OFS leg is explicitly zero**; a MISSING ofs leg is an unanswered question, not a zero. The other three (board, mechanism, exchanges) are labels; an absent one shortens the row rather than printing a chip that says nothing.
- **THE LOT LADDER REPLACED THE "Lot Size" / "Min Application" TILES AND LOST NOTHING** (operator ask, 17 Oct): the lot size IS the first row's share count and the minimum application IS its amount, and the two bands above retail were gained — which is the question a reader with ₹5 lakh actually has. `lotLadder()` returns a MIN and a MAX row per category on mainboard and ONE per category on SME; the panel collapses those to a row each with a range, because five rows is a table and three is a glance in a ~430px column. **The collapsing is presentation only — the arithmetic stays in the shared helper where its tests are.** The top band is open-ended and says so with a `+`, read from the band's own label (`/^above/i`) and not from its position, so a ladder that ends differently cannot be given a ceiling it does not have.
  - A real `<table>` with a `<th scope="row">` per category: four columns of related figures with a header row IS a table, and a grid of divs would read to a screen reader as a pile of text.
  - It added 204px and made a 768-tall screen scroll by 6px. **The BOARD drives the panel height, not the stage** (720 vs 605 measured), so the space came out of the board's own rhythm rather than the ladder's type size — a table of figures that has to be squinted at is worth less than the 12px it would save. 720 → 673.
- **PRINT FORMS GAVE ITS SLOT TO APPLY, and Apply still reads `extra.startBid`.** The IPO card and the detail page both still offer Print Forms, so nothing was lost; this is the consumer home page, where Apply is the action. **The gate is NOT bypassed**: with Start Bid off, `/apply/<symbol>` renders *"Bidding hasn't started yet"*, and a button that lands there is worse than no button — so the panel shows "View IPO details" alone until the toggle is on.
- **THE OPERATOR GATES ARE NOW READ LIVE, which they were not.** `HomeView` is a SERVER component — `await getIpos()` runs at BUILD time — so the panel was reading a snapshot: turning Start Bid on would change the IPO card and the detail page (both re-fetch on mount) while this panel went on offering "View IPO details" **until someone happened to redeploy**. One `getIpoDetail(symbol)` on open, merged over the baked record; a failed fetch leaves the baked one in place. **A toggle whose effect depends on a deploy is a toggle nobody can trust.**
  - The merged record is `useMemo`d, and not for speed: the open effect lists `ipo` in its dependencies, so a fresh object literal every render would re-run it every render. It survived only because both `setState` calls in that effect are no-ops once settled, which is not a property to depend on.
  - **PROVED FROM THE API'S OWN ACCESS LOG, not from the code.** This box cannot reach `newipoapi.finwave.co` (NAT hairpinning), so the browser's own network panel shows only timeouts and proves nothing. Render headless with **`--host-resolver-rules="MAP newipoapi.finwave.co 127.0.0.1"`**, then grep `C:\inetpub\logs\LogFiles\W3SVC8\u_ex*.log` for the request: `/api/ipos/by-symbol/JPL` appeared at the render's timestamp. **That technique is the way to exercise any client-side fetch from this machine.**
  - **The Apply BRANCH itself is unproven end to end** — it needs `startBid: true`, which is the operator's toggle and was not flipped to test. It is the same `ex.startBid === true` expression `IpoCard` has shipped for months.
- **THE KEY-DATE STRIP REPLACED THE "Bidding window" LINE, which was the three-restatements fault coming back.** Anchor · Opens · Closes · Allotment · Listing, with step states derived from TODAY (`done` / `next` / plain) so the strip cannot describe a stage the issue has left. Opens and Closes appear in it, so the window line below it would have been the third time a reader met those two dates — it now renders only as the FALLBACK for a record whose timeline lacks both.
- **The strapline on the panel is *"Your IPO Partner"*, which is NOT the brand tagline recorded at the top of this file** (*"Investing in IPOs, has never been this easy."*). It came from the team's comp and was used as supplied; worth reconciling before it spreads to a second surface.
- **THE COUNTDOWN REPLACED THREE WAYS OF SAYING ONE THING, which is most of why the first cut read flat**: an *"Opens in 4 days"* chip beside an Opens tile and a Closes tile. One ticking clock to the opening bell says it once and gives the board a focal point. It anchors on **10:00** exactly as `OpensIn` in `IpoCard` already does, so the hour is the codebase's existing convention and not precision invented for a popup — a D:H:M countdown to a *date-only* field would have been false precision. Client-only by construction (nothing until the first effect runs): a time rendered on the server is already wrong when it arrives, and React reports the difference as a hydration mismatch.
- **THE RUNNER-UP IS DELIBERATELY NOT NAMED, and that is a data finding rather than a design one.** The obvious way to dramatise "biggest" is a comparison, and the catalogue ranks **JPL 30,213 · PAYTM 28,300 · HYUNDAI 27,870.16**. But **our stored Paytm figure has NO legs, no share count and nothing else to corroborate it**, and 28,300 is one transposed digit from the ₹18,300 Cr that issue is published at (₹8,300 Cr fresh + ₹10,000 Cr OFS). **A comparison is only worth printing when BOTH sides of it are sound** — and the superlative holds under either reading, so naming second place would have risked a wrong number on a public page to add nothing. **PAYTM's `issueSizeCr` should be checked against its RHP.**
- **JPL's own figure IS self-corroborating, which is why it can be set in 46px type**: `2,70,00,000 shares × ₹1,119 = ₹30,213 Cr` exactly, from the record's own fresh leg.
- **THE COUNT-UP RENDERS THE TRUE FIGURE FIRST.** `CountUp` initialises state at the FINAL number and the ramp only overwrites it transiently, so a browser that never runs the effect, one reporting prefers-reduced-motion, and a frame dropped mid-ramp all leave the correct figure on screen. Every other animation on this panel is decoration; this one draws a NUMBER, and a count-up that can strand a wrong one would be the single thing on the page that lies. `useLayoutEffect`, not `useEffect` — the reset to zero must land before paint. **It waits out the reveal**: `.fi-amt` carries an entry delay, so a ramp started on mount spends most of itself behind `opacity: 0`.
- **AMBIENCE IS SLOW AND DIM; THE CELEBRATION FIRES ONCE.** The aurora (three radial washes drifting on an 18s alternate cycle) and 18 rising embers are atmosphere — the burst is the only thing that reads as applause, and it does not loop, because a celebration that never ends starts to read as a promise. **The embers are DETERMINISTIC, not random**: `Math.random()` differs between the server render and the client one, which React reports as a hydration mismatch, so every value is a function of the index.
- **THE HEADLINE'S GOLD SHINE IS GUARDED BY `@supports`, AND THAT GUARD IS THE WHOLE REASON IT COULD BE ADDED.** `.fi-big` keeps a SOLID gold as its base; only inside `@supports ((-webkit-background-clip: text) or (background-clip: text))` does the inner `<span>` become a clipped moving gradient. Clipping unconditionally renders **nothing at all** where it is unsupported, and an invisible headline is a far worse failure than a flat one. Both ends of the sweep rest on gold, so the parked state is a clean gold headline rather than a half-lit one.
- **EVERY NEW `::after` / `::before` ANIMATION HAD TO BE STOPPED BY NAME.** The global reduced-motion reset is `* { animation: none !important }` and **`*` does not match pseudo-elements**. Measured in headless Chrome, which reports `reduce`: aurora off, embers `display: none`, shine parked at `background-position: 0 0` (flat gold), CTA sweep parked at `left: -60%` off the button.
- **`scrollHeight − clientHeight` IS THE WRONG OVERFLOW TEST ON A PANEL WITH A DECORATIVE LAYER.** `.fi-aurora` is deliberately `inset: -34%`, so `.fi-head` reports a **179px overrun that nothing can see** — exactly 34% of its 527px height. Measure the **lowest painted TEXT LEAF** against the box instead; that reads 128px of slack in the stage and 22px in the board.
- **Verify against BOTH stylesheets the home page references.** It carries two (12 KB and ~266 KB); the `.fi-*` rules are in the large one, and checking the first match gives a clean false negative.
- **The browser pane CANNOT reach `investoyard.com` from this box** — NAT hairpinning, the same reason the domain check was rewritten. Verify a deploy with `curl -sk --resolve investoyard.com:443:192.168.46.88` and grep the published CSS and JS chunks for the authored strings.
- **`EISDIR` ON THE EXPORT RECURRED TWICE AND WAS TRANSIENT BOTH TIMES.** `find .next/server/app -type d \( -name '*.html' -o -name '*.rsc' \)` returned **0** while the error named ten `/apply/*.rsc` paths, and a retry succeeded — so **check whether the path is really a directory before clearing `.next`** and paying for a 180 MB webpack-cache rebuild. Note also that a `next build` can **STALL**: one sat at 8s of CPU over 14 minutes with an empty log and had to be killed.

## Sir's eight web reports, 2026-10-20 — and the demo store most of them came from

- **THREE OF THE EIGHT WERE ONE CAUSE: `apps/web/lib/store.ts` WAS A LOCALSTORAGE MOCK OF THE WHOLE PRODUCT, AND SIGNING IN RAN IT.** `store.signIn()` minted three fake applicants into every browser — **"Rahul Sharma", PAN `ABCDE1234F`, a fake NSDL demat, `kycStatus: verified`** — `placeApplication()` fabricated an application with a real-looking `IY…` number, and `deriveLifecycle()` walked a fake pipeline on a 12-second timer to a **coin-flip allotment and an invented listing gain, both keyed on a hash of the application id**. Deleted. Applicants and applications come from the API only; `load()` spreads over `empty` so the demo rows already sitting in a customer's browser are dropped on the next visit.
  - **Point 3, the permanent "RS" avatar, WAS Rahul Sharma.** `SiteHeader` took the initials of the first profile whose relationship was `self` — the demo person — so every signed-in customer on the site wore his initials. It reads the ACCOUNT HOLDER's own name now, and **`SessionCheck` adopts that name from the SERVER**: `/auth/me` already returned it and that call already happens on every page load, so a name corrected in admin is right on the next load and everyone who signed in before names were kept is backfilled for free.
  - **THE DANGEROUS HALF WAS NOT THE AVATAR.** `ApplyWizard`'s live branch needed `liveId && hasToken`, so ANY failure of `getIpoDetail()` — a timeout, a 500, an expired session — fell through to the fabricated path and showed the SAME green "Application placed" screen. An investor was told their bid was in and it existed nowhere. **That is the identical fault removed from mobile on 2026-10-09**, surviving on web because the rule was applied to one surface and the other was never grepped. It refuses honestly now and names which half is missing.
  - **"Use sample details" wrote a fake PAN and demat onto a REAL applicant**, one button press from the apply screen. Gone; the only action offered is the one that fills them in properly.
  - **`kycStatus` IS A DEAD COLUMN — only ever written `'unverified'`** — so it can never be the "Verified" sir asked for in point 5. The demo profiles were the only thing in the product that ever claimed to be verified.
- **POINT 2 — bHNI's applications-for-1× WAS ~4.8× TOO HIGH, and the fix is a SECOND helper, not a change to the first.** `minApplicationShares()` is the minimum **BID**: ICDR puts bHNI above ₹10 L and that is right for the lot ladder. Sir's rule is about the minimum **ALLOTMENT** — *"sHNI gets minimum 1 lot and bHNI gets minimum lot of 2 lakhs plus"* — so both NII bands are allotted the same parcel, and the column counting how many applicants a quota can serve must divide by THAT. `minAllotmentLots()` / `minAllotmentShares()` sit beside the bid helper with 6 tests; **`lotLadder()` deliberately still reads the bid one.** Two different questions wearing the same shape — exactly the offered-rounds/allocation-floors and ceil-vs-floor splits already documented here.
  - **The 2:1 arithmetic corroborates it.** NII is 10% bHNI / 5% sHNI, so on one parcel bHNI needs exactly **twice** sHNI's applications — JPL comes out 1,34,980 against 67,490, exact. The old basis gave bHNI **26,996**, i.e. FEWER applications off double the quota, which is the tell that it was wrong.
  - **IT DISAGREES WITH ONE PUBLISHED RHP AND THAT IS FLAGGED, NOT ABSORBED.** SAIURJA's own prospectus prints bHNI **21**; this makes it **56**. The RHP figure is reproduced by the BID basis, so either SME minimum allotment differs from sir's mainboard rule or that RHP column answers a different question. **Sir's rule is in force because he stated it; the divergence is his to reverse.**
- **POINT 7 WAS AN ORDERING PROBLEM, NOT A CONTENTS PROBLEM, and getting that right is what saved the feature.** "Shown on indian bank, not out of India bank" reads as a request to filter foreign banks out — but the directory is RBI's own IFSC list, every bank in it HAS Indian branches, and Citi / Standard Chartered / DBS India are real ASBA banks. **Removing any of them makes a valid application impossible to enter.** What sir was actually looking at: ordered by name, the first twelve options were an army credit co-operative and eleven district co-operative banks, while **STATE BANK OF INDIA sat at #1163 of 1348.**
  - **Measured before designing: 1,068 of the 1,348 banks have exactly ONE branch and 46 have over 500**, so an alphabetical list is almost entirely long tail with everything anyone banks with buried in it.
  - **TWO TIERS, because the two groups are reached differently.** The 72 banks with 100+ branches are reached by LOOKING and are ordered biggest first (SBI 26,483 · PNB 13,048 · Canara 12,155 · HDFC 10,737 …); the 1,276-bank tail is reached by TYPING and stays alphabetical. A single size-ordered list leaves a co-operative bank's position meaningless; a single alphabetical one is the bug. **The threshold is calibrated against the distribution** — 100 leaves a clean break above the 136-bank 11-to-100 bucket.
  - **`lower(b.name)`, not `b.name`** — Postgres's collation sorts all-caps acronyms before mixed case, putting `CITI Bank` ahead of `Calicut Co-operative`. 16 such pairs; a human expects case-insensitive.
  - **`SearchOption.group` was added so the ordering explains itself.** Without a heading the jump back to "A" at the boundary reads as the list having restarted. The heading is a SIBLING and is NOT focusable — `hl` indexes `filtered`, so anything consuming an index there breaks keyboard navigation.
- **POINT 5 — "Verified" COMES FROM THE EXCHANGE AND NOTHING ELSE**, per sir's answer. `/appdpstatus` is the depository confirming this PAN owns this demat; nothing else on the platform establishes it.
  - **IT READS A TIMESTAMP, NOT THE STATUS, and that is the whole design.** `status` holds only the CURRENT value, so an application that has moved on to `mandate_pending` or `allotted` no longer says `dp_verified` while the verification plainly did happen. `dpVerifiedAt` comes from `ApplicationStatusEvent`, narrowed to that one event in `list()`'s include and selected WITH `status` so one expression in `toView` also serves `getOne()`, which includes every event.
  - **ONE CHIP FOR BOTH FIELDS, because it is one event.** Two ticks would imply two confirmations.
  - **`--pos-ink` on `--pos-soft`, never `--pos`** — the obvious pairing is **3.50:1 and fails AA**, the measurement already recorded here. **`.st.ok` still carries that pairing**; changing it reaches every admin report chip and is its own job.
  - **NOTHING WILL SHOW VERIFIED YET** — 0 status events and 0 exchange application numbers exist, because the rail waits on NSE UAT. The marker renders when the first one arrives. Web and mobile both carry it, kept in step by hand.
- **TWO DEAD SURFACES FOUND WHILE AUDITING, BOTH CAUSED BY A STUB THAT SILENTLY ANSWERED "NOTHING".** `mockIpoBySymbol()` was kept as a no-op after the mock catalogue was deleted, so its callers would still compile.
  - **THE WATCHLIST TAB WAS EMPTY FOR EVERYONE WHO HAD EVER STARRED AN IPO.** It looked each symbol up through that stub, got `undefined`, and hit `if (!ipo) return null` — while the server held their list perfectly well. It reads the real catalogue now, fetched only when the tab is opened.
  - **The application card's Allotment/Listing line never rendered either**, same cause. The two dates now ride on the application itself.
  - **A stub that answers "nothing" is worse than a missing function, which the compiler would have named.** Both deleted.
- **POINT 4 — the confirmation is a STEP, not a toast.** Verifying used to `router.push()` in the same tick, so the account was created and the person was simply somewhere else with nothing having said so. It does **NOT** auto-advance: a timed redirect puts the reader back to wondering whether it worked, which is the complaint. The stale *"nothing is needed to create the account"* fine print is hidden once it exists.
- **POINT 6 — signing up was reachable ONLY through the no-account banner**, i.e. only by someone already turned away. A door has to be visible before you need it. Hidden once that banner is up, because two offers to sign up stacked read as a mistake.
- **POINT 8 reuses the page's EXISTING `msg` banner** (which the delete path already writes to) rather than adding a toast: a second place a result can appear makes the scroll-to-top pointless. `onSaved` carries the name up so the message says who.
- **POINT 1 — the mobile menu's scrim was 375×63 and its sheet sat at `top: -502`.** `backdrop-filter` on `.header` **creates a containing block for `position: fixed` descendants**, so a full-screen sheet was being laid out inside the 63px header. Portalled to `document.body` now — the house pattern `Tooltip` / `SearchSelect` / `Modal` already use for `overflow:hidden` parents. After: scrim **375×812**, sheet 375×565 at y=247 (flush to the bottom edge), parent `BODY`, all 14 links on-screen.
  - **`/subscription` ALSO SCROLLED 31px SIDEWAYS, AND THE OBVIOUS FIX DOES NOTHING.** `.subv2-seg` already had `overflow-x: auto` and never scrolled: it sat **382px wide inside a 327px parent with `scrollWidth === clientWidth`**, i.e. it had GROWN. It is a flex item of a COLUMN container, so its width is the CROSS axis — **`flex-shrink` does not apply, and `align-items: stretch` will not shrink a scroll container below its content either** (tried in the live DOM: still 382px). **`min-width: 0`, the reflex fix, changed nothing — measured.** `max-width: 100%` is the lever: 327px and scrolling internally. `max-width` rather than `width`, so on a wider phone the pills are not stretched to fill the row.
  - **Swept all 18 consumer pages at 375px by measuring `scrollWidth − clientWidth` in a 375px same-origin iframe** (media queries respond to the iframe's own viewport, so one call measures them all). 18 of 18 clean afterwards.
- **`mapApiProfile` HAD TO MOVE before Portfolio could use it.** It lived inside `ApplyWizard`, and a second copy in Portfolio is how two readings of one API row drift apart. It is in `lib/store.ts` beside the `Profile` type it produces.
- **THE STAGED BUILD EARNED ITS KEEP TWICE MORE IN ONE AFTERNOON, with two NEW export races — and the cure is to clear `out-staging` YOURSELF.** `ENOENT: mkdir out-staging\hi\ipos\ACETEC` (a parallel mkdir race — the directory existed moments later) and then **`ENOTEMPTY: rmdir out-staging\print`, which is the exact failure that took the site down for 25 minutes on 2026-10-16**, now landing harmlessly in staging. `out/` measured intact at 5,644,688 bytes / 1,005 ipo pages through both. A `Remove-Item -Recurse` before the build removes Next's own clear step from the equation and it then went green first time, three builds running.
- **THE HARNESS EATS A BACKSLASH IN A HEREDOC, AND IT COST THREE ROUND TRIPS AGAIN.** `/^\d{10}$/` landed as `/^d{10}$/` in `LoginForm` — a login that would have rejected every valid number — and the `\s+` in `SiteHeader` made an exact-match patch fail on a file that looked identical. It also swallowed a whole `cat > file <<'MD'` twice. **Three things that work, all cheaper than fighting it**: patch AROUND the backslash-bearing line rather than through it, prefer an expression needing none (`mobile.length === 10` — the input is already digit-filtered), and use the Write tool for whole files. Then **grep every file touched** for the shape; this file has now recorded the same trap three times.

## Visitor tracking, and the six operator reports beside it (2026-10-21)
- **THE BEACON IS THE ONLY WAY A PAGE VIEW CAN BE KNOWN HERE, and that is a consequence of the static export.** IIS serves `out/` — there is no server render, no middleware, nothing of ours in the request path — so `POST /api/metrics/view` from the browser is it. The endpoint is PUBLIC and has to be; what keeps that safe is that **nothing a caller sends reaches storage unfiltered**: the path is checked against a server-held `TRACKED_PATHS` whitelist, the symbol is stripped to exchange characters, and a visitor is `sha256(daily salt + ip + ua)` truncated.
  - **THE SALT LIVES IN REDIS, NOT IN THE PROCESS.** Two workers with different salts hash the same person two ways and double the day's unique count. `SET NX` means the first worker to ask wins and the rest adopt it; with Redis down it degrades to a per-process salt rather than failing, which is the right posture for a figure nobody acts on.
  - **ONE REQUEST IS BOTH THE VIEW AND THE HEARTBEAT**, and it returns the live count, so a reader parked on the page costs one small request every 30 s rather than two. **It stops on `visibilitychange`** — a tab left open all afternoon would otherwise read as present all afternoon and the live figure would drift up from people who had left. `requestAnimationFrame` is NOT an option for this; it does not fire in a hidden tab at all.
  - **PRESENCE IS A SORTED SET, never a key per visitor.** Counting the second shape needs `SCAN`, which is O(keys) across the whole database on a path anybody on the internet can hit. `ZADD` + `ZREMRANGEBYSCORE` + `ZCARD` is O(log n) and the trim pays for itself. Bots are excluded from it — a crawler is not a person, and this number is shown to readers.
  - **THE LIVE LINE ONLY APPEARS AT THREE OR MORE** (operator's call). Measured first: the busiest day on these pages was **59 distinct visitors spread over a whole day**, so the concurrent figure is nearly always 0–2, and *"1 person viewing now"* is the reader themselves. Below the threshold it says nothing. **The number is never padded to reach it.**
  - **`symbol` IS AN EMPTY STRING AND NEVER NULL.** Postgres treats NULLs as DISTINCT in a unique index, so a nullable column there makes the upsert insert a fresh row on every beacon instead of incrementing — the counter would read 1 for ever.
  - **The report's Total is DISTINCT over the range, not the column sum, and the page says so.** A visitor seen in three hours is three rows and one person. Across DAYS the two agree, because the salt rotates at IST midnight and yesterday's key can never equal today's. A total that quietly disagrees with its own column is a bug; one that explains itself is a measurement.
- **THE IP AND THE SIGNED-IN IDENTITY WERE ADDED ON OPERATOR REQUEST, AND THEY ARE THE ONLY PERSONAL DATA ON THE ROW** — everything else is an irreversible hash and a counter. **90-day retention, then CLEARED IN PLACE** (`METRICS_PII_DAYS`): `purgeOldPii` is an `updateMany` setting `ip: ''` and `userId: null`, **never a delete**, so every figure in the report survives the sweep and what expires is only the part that would otherwise accumulate for ever. It runs on boot and every six hours, `unref`'d so it never holds the process open, and is scoped to rows that still hold something so a re-run is a no-op. **The admin screen states the window where the addresses are read**, not only in a policy document.
  - **`subjectOf` IS LENIENT WHERE `JwtAuthGuard` IS STRICT, and the difference is the whole point.** A missing, malformed or expired token means ANONYMOUS, never a 401 — the route is public and must stay public, a page view is not worth refusing, and refusing would also tell a caller which tokens are good. The signature is still verified, so an identity cannot be asserted without the secret. **No database lookup on the public path**: the id is stored as sent and resolved to a name only when the report is read. The ACCOUNT STATE is deliberately not checked either — a suspended reader looking at a public page is still a reader, and this is a traffic log, not an authorisation decision.
  - **`userId` IS SET ON UPDATE AS WELL AS CREATE.** A reader can sign in part-way through an hour and their visitor key does not change (same address, same browser), so writing it only on create would keep the anonymous first impression and the sign-in would never appear. It is only ever set there and never cleared — clearing is the purge's job alone.
  - **A signed-in reader shows as a NAME PLUS THE LAST FOUR of their mobile**, the message log's shape, which keeps this a support tool for recognising someone rather than a second copy of the contact list.
  - **`windowOf()` IS THE ONE WINDOW RESOLVER** shared by `report` and `visits`. A detail list that forms its own opinion of what the filters meant is a second opinion about the summary above it — the same reasoning that gives the allotment list and its CSV one `recordWhere()`.
