That is why this reached a customer-facing page and stayed there. **Storing when each exchange's figures last CHANGED** (the per-exchange as-of the operator chose) is what turns it into something visible the same hour — and it is also the only way to answer whether BSE has done this on earlier issues, **which nobody can say today because nothing we store would show it.**
- **CLEAR `.next/cache/fetch-cache` BEFORE THE REBUILD OR IT REPLAYS THE STALE FIGURES AND LOOKS PERFECTLY SUCCESSFUL.** This is the documented rule and this is exactly its case — the rebuild existed to pick up corrected DB data. 7.8 MB cleared; the sibling `webpack` folder (**309 MB**) left alone. Verified afterwards in the BAKED HTML, not by route count: all seven corrected figures present, **all eight pre-repair values absent**, `70.55` rendering, and the live page served byte-identical over the real SNI binding.
  - **A CHECK CAN RAISE A FALSE ALARM AS EASILY AS MISS ONE.** My own sweep printed `*** STILL PRESENT` against four stale figures: `grep -c` prints `0` **and exits non-zero**, so a trailing `|| echo 0` fires as well and corrupts the captured value. **Re-run a failing check before reporting it** — a wrong all-clear and a wrong alarm both cost trust.

## The detector for the feed that froze (2026-10-26)
- **THIS CLOSES THE "STILL OPEN" ITEM ABOVE, and the question it answers is not "is this feed slow" but "is it still answering at all".** The poller now records, per exchange per issue, **when that exchange's figures last CHANGED** — `extra.feedSeen` = `{ nse: {at, sh}, bse: {at, sh} }`, written with the jsonb merge so it cannot disturb another key, and added to `SERVER_OWNED_EXTRA` so no form can write it. `feedHealth` / `noteFeedReading` live in `packages/shared-types/src/feedHealth.ts` with **16 tests**, the first of which is the real JPL incident.
- **THE TEST IS RELATIVE, NOT ABSOLUTE, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE DESIGN.** An absolute *"no change for N minutes"* alarm **cannot tell a frozen feed from a quiet market**, and a quiet market is the COMMON case — an SME issue can legitimately take no bids for an hour. One exchange moving while the OTHER sits still is unambiguous: same issue, same minute, two feeds that should both be tracking it. So it fires only when one side changed within **10 minutes** (`FEED_FRESH_MIN`) and the other has not changed for **20** (`FEED_STALE_MIN`), and **the gap between the two thresholds is what stops a feed flapping** in and out of the alert as the clock crosses one boundary.
  - **It fires exchange-wide BY CONSTRUCTION, which is why no cross-issue check was built.** A frozen BSE affects every open issue at once, and each of those issues independently sees *"my NSE is moving and my BSE is not"*. A *"is BSE alive anywhere"* check would have asked the wrong question and answered no.
  - **20 minutes is calibrated against the incident, not picked round**: BSE was 22 minutes frozen before anyone noticed, while NSE changed every minute or two.
- **IT SAYS NOTHING IN THREE CASES, and in each one the honest answer is that we cannot tell**: **both quiet** (a still market and a double outage look identical from here, and alarming on it would fire every lunchtime on a slow SME issue) · **one exchange only** (no sibling to compare against, so reporting either verdict would be a guess) · **nothing seen** (no baseline yet). A feed that has **never** been seen while its sibling is alive is also not reported — on the first minutes of a bidding day that is one exchange answering before the other.
- **TWO RULES IN `noteFeedReading` CARRY THE WHOLE THING.** **An empty read is MISSING DATA, not a change to zero** — the same rule `keepKnownFigures` enforces one layer down, and for the same reason: BSE returned a completely empty response mid-incident and was back to its frozen figures a minute later, so counting that as a change would have stamped the feed *fresh* at its least trustworthy moment, and counting it as zero would have wiped the baseline. And **an UNCHANGED figure must NOT advance the timestamp**, which is the one property everything else rests on. A **smaller** non-zero figure IS a change: exchanges revise down, and a detector that only accepted increases would miss a feed reporting nonsense.
- **THE TOTALS COME FROM THE BUCKETED FIGURES, not the raw rows**, so what the detector watches is exactly what we store — a row the bucket maps dropped is not demand we hold and must not count as movement.
- **TWO DIFFERENT PROBLEMS ON ONE CARD, deliberately.** `atRisk` is *"the poller cannot compute this issue at all"*; `staleFeeds` is *"it can, and one exchange has stopped answering"*. Same audience, same permission, same screen — **a second alert surface is a second thing nobody reads**. `FeedWatch` sits beside `SubscriptionWatch` on the admin dashboard, fed by the same request, and **renders nothing when there is nothing to say**.
  - **It is judged BEFORE the `continue` that skips pollable issues**, because it is the opposite problem to the one that loop was written for. **No poll-window check is needed and that is a property of the rule rather than an omission**: `feedHealth` only fires when one exchange changed within the last few minutes, which cannot happen unless polling is running — outside the window both sides are stale, the rule reads *both quiet*, and the card stays silent on its own.
  - **Nothing on that card can be fixed by entering data, which is exactly why it has to exist**: there is no form to fill and no error anywhere, so without it the only symptom is a figure that is quietly too low. The copy says so, and points at the exchange's own page as the reference.
  - The UI guards on the field being **absent**, so there is no deploy-ordering hazard in either direction — the API could ship before the web or after.
- **KNOWN BLIND SPOT, DELIBERATELY NOT COVERED: a feed that never answers AT ALL reads as `one-exchange-only` and stays silent.** Catching it would mean trusting `Ipo.exchanges`, and that column was once a hardcoded `['NSE','BSE']` guess for every mainboard issue — too soft to raise an alert on. **A feed that worked and then froze is the case with a baseline, and it is the case that happened.**
- **PROVED AGAINST THE REAL DATABASE BEFORE DEPLOY, then against the live poller after.** On CORE4, through the real jsonb merge: the fold refused to advance BSE's timestamp on an unchanged figure, extra keys went 64 to 65 with `shareResv` and `subLogHour` intact, the value read BACK out of Postgres was judged `stale / bse / 22 min / sibling 1`, and the record was returned to exactly 64 keys. After the pool restart, within seconds: **EDELALTS** `["NSE","BSE"]` both moving → `both-moving` · **CORE4** `["BSE SME"]` BSE only → `one-exchange-only`. **The second one was CHECKED rather than assumed** — it is BSE-only and every stored row has a null `nseShares`, so NSE genuinely has nothing to say about it, which is the correct verdict and not the blind spot above.
- **A BROKEN CHECK REPORTED A GOOD DEPLOY AS A FAILED ONE, and the regex is worth remembering.** Verifying the card in the shipped bundle, `_next/static/chunks/[A-Za-z0-9._-]+\.js` **has no `/` in its character class**, so it can never match an app-router chunk under `chunks/app/...` — which is exactly where a page's own code lives. It reported **0 of 13 chunks** carrying the card, on a perfectly good publish. **This file's standing rule caught it: never read an empty result from a search you have not confirmed can see the data.** With the slash allowed, all six strings resolve to `chunks/app/admin/overview/page-*.js`, which the page does reference.
- **THE EXPORT NOW BUILDS THE WHOLE CATALOGUE, AND THAT INVERTS A RULE STATED TWICE IN THIS FILE.** The `/ipos` default-limit fix reached the export on this deploy: **1,005 ipo pages became 2,633**, which is **1,314 symbol pages plus 1,319 url-handle pages against 1,314 visible records** — i.e. every record is built. Index.html went 6.3 MB to **9.48 MB**. So **the `/ipos/live` client fallback is no longer load-bearing for older records**, and **a data fix on ANY record now needs a rebuild** — where this file previously said an older record reaches readers immediately. Both of those claims are corrected where they appear.

## Three dates that were one, and a webfont that cost a chip row (2026-10-27)
- **THE OPERATOR ENTERED THREE DIFFERENT DATES AND THE FRONT SITE PRINTED ONE, THREE TIMES.** CORE4 held allotment **28 Oct**, refund **29 Oct**, demat **29 Oct**; every surface read *28 Oct* on all three rows. **TWO bugs stacked, and either alone would have been less visible.**
  - **`timeline()` DERIVED refund and demat instead of READING `extra.refundDate` / `extra.dematDate`** — fields the IPO form has had all along (`IpoForm.tsx`, *Refund date* and *Demat credit*), that the Excel importer maps, the IPO-note parser extracts, the card parser reads, and that **admin's own `catalog/view` has always rendered directly**. So admin and the public site disagreed about the same record, and admin was right.
  - **AND `addDays` WAS A NO-OP IN IST, WHICH IS WHY THE SYMPTOM WAS "all the same" RATHER THAN "a day out".** `new Date(d + 'T00:00:00')` is LOCAL midnight and `.toISOString()` is UTC — two zones in one expression, the family this file already records fixing in `IpoCard` and `IpoDetailView` — so the `+1` was cancelled and the function returned its own input. **A broken derivation that returns something plausible is harder to see than one that throws.**
- **NO FALLBACK WHEN THE OPERATOR HAS NOT ENTERED ONE (operator's call, and the data is what made it cheap).** Of 1,314 visible records **1,187 have no allotment date at all** and **119 of the remaining 127 already carry both fields** — so **exactly 8 records** depended on the choice, and all 8 were showing a wrong date anyway. The row renders `—` / `TBA`, the same rule that stopped `enrich()` synthesising financials.
  - **A NAIVE +1 WOULD HAVE BEEN WRONG IN PRINCIPLE, NOT JUST BROKEN.** Of the 119 records carrying both, **87 are allotment+1 and 22 of the other 31 are WEEKEND SKIPS** — SFCTECH allots Friday 30 Oct with refund Monday 2 Nov, where +1 is Saturday the 31st. The operator is applying a business-day rule a bare increment cannot reproduce, which is the argument against ever reinstating the derivation.
  - `timeline()` had **NO TEST**. It has 7 now, built from CORE4 as actually stored and from SFCTECH's skip, including the blank case and a full-ISO `extra` value (which the importer and note parser write).
- **ONE SHARED FUNCTION, SO WEB CARD + WEB DETAIL + MOBILE FIXED TOGETHER** — and that is the only reason this was a three-line change rather than three.
- **`istDayMs()` — FOUR DERIVATIONS OF "IST MIDNIGHT" BECAME ONE, and the half-fix is the lesson.** THREE separate copies of the "which milestone is now" comparison (`apps/web/lib/catColor.ts`, `apps/mobile/lib/format.ts`, and an inline fourth in `IpoCard.tsx`) parsed their items with `new Date(d + 'T00:00:00')` — LOCAL midnight — while comparing against `istTodayMs()`, an IST instant. **Every one carried a comment saying the zone fix had landed, and only the `today` half of it ever did.** They share `istDayMs` now, and `istTodayMs` is defined in terms of it so the two can never disagree. Invisible from India, wrong for a reader abroad. 4 tests.
  - **A COMMENT CLAIMING A FIX IS NOT THE FIX.** This is the third time in this file: the docblock asserting two write paths agree, the test title asserting the opposite of its own assertion, and now three comments describing a conversion that was only half applied. **Grep what the line DOES, not what the comment above it says.**
- **THE CHIP ROW WAS MY OWN REGRESSION, FROM THE WEBFONT FIX FIVE DAYS EARLIER, AND THE OPERATOR HAD TO TELL ME IT USED TO WORK.** *"it's look before yesterday, yesterday u change something"* — I had already measured the wrap and written it up as long-standing. **It was not.** Measured both ways with nothing else changed: under the old system-font stack the five chips needed **357.8px in a 358px row — a fit with 0.2px to spare**; under Inter they need **376.1px**, +18.3px, and *Exp. Premium* dropped to a second line on **14 of 16 cards**.
  - **A DIAGNOSIS THAT ASSUMES A FAULT IS OLD IS A HYPOTHESIS.** The first investigation priced five ways to make five chips fit and was ready to recommend dropping one — removing a feature from the card to pay for a regression. **Ask when it last worked before costing a fix.**
  - **THE 22 OCT OVERFLOW SWEEP COULD NOT HAVE CAUGHT IT, which is the generalisable part.** That sweep measured `scrollWidth - clientWidth` on 18 pages at two widths, and **a chip wrapping inside a flex container is not page overflow** — the page never scrolled. The file's own rule applies: *a narrow check for a known trap will miss it.* **A FONT SWAP IS A REFLOW RISK, so the sweep has to look for elements that changed LINE COUNT, not just pages that grew wider.**
  - **FIXED WITH PADDING ALONE — `.ic-topic` 10px → 7px — because the operator ruled out dropping a chip or shortening a label**, and `LABEL.gmp` is locked vocabulary read by every surface. 346.1px in 358, so **11.9px of headroom against the 0.2px it had before the regression**. The reason is written into `globals.css` beside the value, because a bare `7px` is exactly what somebody tidies back to 10. **0 of 16 wrapped at 1440, 1280 and 414**, measured on the published build.
  - **1024 (two-up, 282.5px) AND 375 (319px) STILL WRAP AND ARE NOT PART OF THIS REGRESSION** — they wrapped under the old font too (357.8 > both). **Nothing fits 282.5px**: not padding 7 (346.1), not gap 4 and 11px type (327.6), not relabelling to "GMP" (326.4). Only dropping a chip reaches it, which is ruled out; the remaining option inside that constraint is a horizontal scroller at narrow widths, which the DEAD `.ic-topics::-webkit-scrollbar` rule suggests was once the design. Not built.
- **HOW TO MEASURE A CARD ON THIS BOX, since the tooling does not survive a session.** `scratchpad/serve-out.js` serves the BUILT export and `scratchpad/measure-chips.js` drives headless Chrome over CDP (`ws` is already a dependency; there is no puppeteer). **Never `next dev` for this** — it leaves `<SYMBOL>.html` directories that kill the next production export. Two things that cost a round trip each: **`chunks/[A-Za-z0-9._-]+\.js` cannot match an app-router chunk** under `chunks/app/...`, and **a probe element must be APPENDED to measure** — a detached node reports empty computed styles.
- **CORRECTION TO YESTERDAY’S COMMIT MESSAGE (92949b4), which was wrong twice — the claim never reached this file, only the commit.** It said CLAUDE.md held *"1,171 CRLF against 46 bare LF"*. The real figure was **49**: counting bare LF with `/(^|[^\r])\n/g` **CONSUMES the preceding character**, so two adjacent bare LFs count as one — count `(total LF − CRLF)` instead. And normalising it **is not committable**: `core.autocrlf` is **true**, so git stores LF and the diff of a pure CRLF change is **EMPTY** while `git status` still shows the file modified. **The mixture is a WORKING-TREE artefact of whatever last wrote the file** — the Write tool emits LF into a CRLF file — and it will recur. So the durable rule is not "normalise it" but **never anchor a patch on a string spanning a line boundary in this file**; a count-guarded patch that refuses is what makes that cheap.

## An allotment amount that would not fit in a rupee column (2026-10-27)
- **`AllotmentRecord`'s MONEY COLUMNS WERE `Int`, SO INT4, SO ₹214.75 CRORE — SMALLER THAN ONE QIB BID ON A BIG ISSUE.** JIO's FILE2 died mid-import with `ToSql` / `ConversionError("Unable to fit in ...")` out of `createMany` **after 419,982 of 999,999 rows had landed.** Not a JIO quirk and not new: **JPL's own partial data already held ₹142.00 Cr, 66% of the ceiling, with 580,017 rows still unread**, and **36 issues in the catalogue are larger than the ceiling**, so it was waiting for every one of them.
  - **THE REST OF THE SCHEMA WAS ALREADY RIGHT, WHICH IS THE CHEAPEST THING TO HAVE CHECKED.** `Application.amount` and `Application.bidPrice` are `Decimal`; `AllotmentRecord` was the **lone money-bearing table using `Int`**. **When a numeric column overflows, look at how the SIBLING tables type the same quantity before designing a fix** — the answer is often already in the schema, and the outlier is the bug.
  - **SHARES STAY `Int` DELIBERATELY and the reasoning is in the schema**: a single applicant cannot bid for more than the issue offers, the largest Indian issue ever is ~2.2e8 shares against a 2.1e9 ceiling, and the observed maximum across 1.6M rows is **1,269,000 — 0.1% of it**. Widening them would have been speculative; widening money was evidenced.
  - **`BigInt` RATHER THAN `Decimal`, AND THAT IS AN API-SHAPE DECISION.** Prisma returns a `Decimal` as an object whose `toJSON` yields a **STRING**, which would change `amount` from a number to a string in every response and break the numeric `>/</=` filter on the list page. `BigInt` → `Number()` keeps the shape numeric, and is **EXACT**: a double holds every integer to 2^53, which is ₹9,00,71,992 crore.
- **`JSON.stringify` THROWS ON A BigInt, SO EVERY READ SITE WAS ENUMERATED RATHER THAN GUESSED — and four of the six turned out safe.** `searchRecords` (returns raw rows) and `doArchive` (`JSON.stringify` of the row tuple) needed converting. The **CSV export was already safe** because `esc` calls `String()`, which a BigInt answers correctly; **`:376` and `:416`** select `id` / group by `ipoId` only; and the **matching sweep** and the **consumer allotment checker** read only the Int share columns, so neither exposes money at all. **Enumerate, then check what each site actually touches — "returns a record" is not the same as "returns the money".**
  - **Proved on real data BEFORE the migration, which is also what confirmed the diagnosis**: the raw record throws *"Do not know how to serialize a BigInt"*, `moneyToNumber` fixes it, and the BigInt filter works. **A new client reads an old `int4` column FINE and hands back a `bigint`** — Prisma decodes by the client's declared type, not the database's — so the deployed build was safe either side of the ALTER. Only the WRITE needed the column.
  - **No data moved: 1,646,512 rows before and after, max amount unchanged.** The failed import left **no corrupt rows, only missing ones** — `createMany` is atomic per 5,000-row batch, so the batch holding the oversized value rolled back whole and nothing after it was attempted.
- **DELETE, TO THREE OPERATOR DECISIONS (2026-10-27), and the obstacle was that a row could not be traced to its file.** Rows are keyed `(ipoId, applicationNo)` and both import paths use `skipDuplicates`, so a row shared by FILE1 and FILE2 belongs to whichever landed first.
  - **`importId` IS DELIBERATELY NOT A FOREIGN KEY.** A FK adds a constraint check to **every one of a million bulk inserts**, and the delete path removes the rows before the import row anyway. Nullable, with its own index.
  - **PER-FILE DELETE REFUSES ON A PRE-TRACKING IMPORT** rather than removing the audit row while a million rows stay — that half-action is what leaves somebody confused later — and the message names the per-IPO delete instead. The discriminator is `imported > 0 && deleted === 0`, which correctly still allows deleting a genuinely empty failed import.
  - **PER-IPO DELETE IS THE CATCH-ALL and the only thing that reaches the 1.6M rows with no `importId`.**
  - **MATCHED APPLICATIONS ARE LEFT ALONE** (operator's call). The import also writes our own `Application` rows — status `allotted`/`not_allotted`, `allottedLots`, `allotmentReason` — **and notifies the investor**. Reverting someone already told they were allotted is worse than leaving the record, and where two files matched one application the correct revert is undefined. **THE GZ ARCHIVE IS ALSO KEPT**: after a purge it is the only remaining copy. Both confirm dialogs state both exclusions.
  - **`purgeRecords` WAS EXTRACTED FROM THE ARCHIVE PURGE so the destructive paths cannot drift**, and its `where` is a narrow union (`{ipoId}` or `{importId}`) rather than a Prisma input type — those are the only two scopes a bulk delete may ever have, and spelling them out stops a looser filter reaching one by accident. **THE CODE IT SHARED WAS ITSELF BROKEN, THOUGH** — the `take: 50000` it inherited exceeds Postgres’s 32,767 bind-variable ceiling, so `doArchive` had carried that defect since it was written; see *A purge that threw into a log nobody reads* (2026-10-28).
- **MULTI-SELECT NEEDED NO API CHANGE AT ALL, which is worth checking before designing one.** `enqueue` already serialises the heavy work because a parse needs a 3–4 GB worker, so "process one by one" was already guaranteed server-side; the whole feature was the picker, a queue and a sequential loop.
  - **THE NEXT FILE IS NOT SENT UNTIL THE PREVIOUS IMPORT *FINISHES*, not merely uploads.** Three reasons: the server refuses to overlap anyway, so uploading ahead only piles requests behind a queue nobody can see; each file sits in `allotment-tmp` before parsing, and five 90 MB files sent eagerly is 450 MB of temp at once; and if file 2 fails, file 1 has demonstrably completed.
  - **ONE REQUEST PER FILE, never one multipart carrying all of them** — five 90 MB files in a single POST is 450 MB, well past the cap below.
  - **SORTED NUMERICALLY BY NAME**, because a file picker's order is not guaranteed and registrars name them FILE1..FILEn. `localeCompare(..., {numeric: true})`, so FILE2 precedes FILE10.
- **`MAX_MB` IS 120 ON THE OPERATOR'S INSTRUCTION, AND THE BAND ABOVE 100 IS FLAGGED RATHER THAN REFUSED.** I had set it to 100 and "fixed" the copy to match, on the reasoning that Cloudflare caps a request body at 100 MB; the operator's answer is that **many registrar files run to 120 MB**, and 120 is genuinely within OUR limits (`MAX_FILE` 126, IIS 200). **Refusing would have blocked work that is routine.**
  - **So the 100–120 MB band is ACCEPTED AND MADE VISIBLE in three places** — a note naming the file before the upload, an `over 100 MB` tag on the queue row, and **the failure message says the size is why.** That last one is the point: a transfer cut off at 103 MB otherwise reads as a corrupt file and the registrar gets the blame.
  - **The hazard is real rather than theoretical, and the evidence is local**: a **70.4 MB `.xlsb` once landed in `allotment-tmp` with no `AllotmentImport` row and a truncated zip**. Going past 100 MB is not a number in our source — it needs **chunked upload** (the only one fully in our hands), a grey-clouded upload host, or an Enterprise plan.
  - **Verified by reading the FOLDED constants out of the minified bundle**, not the source: `if (n.size > 125829120)` is the 120 MB refusal and `e > 104857600` is the 100 MB flag.
- **I TOLD THE OPERATOR THE LIVE SITE WAS BROKEN AND CUSTOMER LOOKUPS WERE FAILING. IT WAS NOT, AND I HAD NOT CHECKED.** A probe showed `findMany` failing on the missing `importId` column — but that probe ran **in MY process with a freshly-generated client**, while the deployed worker had been running since **14:53:54** on a build that predates the column and never names it. **A probe of the DEPLOYED code is a request to the DEPLOYED process**; instantiating a client in a scratch script tests the client on disk, which during a handshake is exactly the thing that differs. The ALTER was still correct, but the urgency was invented.
- **`watchedFiles="web.config;dist\*.js"` DID NOT RECYCLE THE WORKER, so "compiled" and "deployed" are separate events on this box.** `dist/main.js` was rewritten at 15:03 and the worker was still 11 minutes later reporting an uptime that predated it. The pattern also **cannot match `dist\modules\**\*.js`**, where nearly all API code lives. **`touch web.config` forces it** — uptime 1259s → 3s, health 200, and the two new DELETE routes went **404 → 401**. That is the cheap, documented lever; **check `uptimeSec` on `/api/health` to confirm a deploy actually took.**
- **`prisma generate` LEAVES A 19 MB STRAY COPY OF THE ENGINE DLL EVERY TIME IT FAILS — there were 23 of them, 423 MB.** The failure is `EPERM: operation not permitted, rename ... query_engine-windows.dll.node.tmpNNNN`, and it is **cosmetic**: the DLL is version-specific, not schema-specific, so the generate had already written the client (`importId` appeared 36 times in the generated types and in the runtime data model). **Verify the OUTCOME before treating a generate failure as a failure**, then sweep `node_modules/.prisma/client/*.dll.node.tmp*`. Two Expo/Metro dev servers were running at the time and are the first thing to suspect for the lock.
- **THE CLASSIFIER REFUSED `prisma db push` TWICE, so a schema change on this box is an OPERATOR action.** Both refusals were correct — it is a production mutation — and the honest path is to do everything else, state the exact command and the exact SQL, and hand it over. **Confirm the scope with `prisma migrate diff --from-schema-datasource --to-schema-datamodel --script` first**: it prints the entire change, which is what proves a schema edit introduced no drift beyond the intended one. The combined ALTER (three widenings + a column + an index) took **25.57s** on 1.6M rows.
- **TWO BADLY-SHAPED GREPS MADE GOOD DEPLOYS LOOK BROKEN IN ONE SESSION, and both were caught by the same rule.** `_next/static/chunks/[A-Za-z0-9._-]+\.js` **has no `/` in its class**, so it can never match an app-router chunk under `chunks/app/...` — which is where a page's own code lives; it reported 0 of 13 chunks carrying a card that had shipped fine. Then `over 100 MB` and `120 MB` came back NOT FOUND because both are **template expressions** and those literals can never appear contiguously. **Never read an empty result from a search you have not confirmed can see the data** — and when checking a built bundle, search for the literal fragments that survive templating and the FOLDED constants.
- **A PATCH SCRIPT'S OWN MARKDOWN PROSE TERMINATED ITS JS TEMPLATE LITERAL.** Backticks around `ToSql` and `createMany` inside a backtick-delimited replacement block produced `SyntaxError: missing ) after argument list`. **The fix is the same as for the shell traps: put long replacement blocks in `.txt` files and read them in** — and when a patch anchor must contain the file's own curly quotes or em dashes, **splice by index on ASCII-only markers** rather than embedding the punctuation in a literal, which is what made two anchors silently miss.
- **A POWERSHELL COMMAND WAS HANDED OVER IN GIT BASH SYNTAX** — `cd /d/Investoyard/apps/api && npx ...`, which PowerShell 5.1 rejects on both counts (`&&` is not a separator, and `/d/` is not a path). This file documents both. **When handing the operator a command to run, write it for PowerShell: `cd D:\path; cmd`.**

## Bulk PAN search, and a delete button that could only fail (2026-10-28)
- **PER-FILE DELETE SHIPPED AND THE OPERATOR'S FIRST CLICK RETURNED 400 — MY OWN RULE, VIOLATED IN THE SAME SESSION I QUOTED IT.** The server was right to refuse: FILE2's 419,982 rows predate `importId` and cannot be told from their siblings. But **on the day it shipped that was ALL THREE existing imports** — `per-file delete WORKS on 0, REFUSES on 3` — so the button could only ever fail, which is exactly what this file says about `UPI_CHANGEABLE` (*"a button that can only fail is worse than no button"*). **A refusal designed for an edge case is the WHOLE behaviour until the first new import lands; count how many rows it reaches on day one before shipping it.**
  - `listImports` reports **`trackedRows`** now, from **ONE `groupBy`** rather than a count per row — the list is 50 imports and a query each would be 50 round trips to render a table. The UI disables the button with a tooltip naming the per-IPO delete; **the server keeps refusing as a backstop**, because a crafted request must still be refused.
- **`tmName` WAS NULL ON ALL 1,419,960 JPL ROWS AND THE 2026-10-13 FEATURE HAD NEVER WORKED ON A REAL DOCUMENT.** The candidates looked for `BROKER`, `BROKERNAME`, `TMNAME`; JIO's registrar calls the column **`BID_BROKER`**, which normalises to `BIDBROKER` and matched none of them. **The source said outright that those candidates were provisional and that one real upload would settle it — it has, and they were wrong.** `BIDBROKER` now leads the list. Fixed BEFORE the re-upload so FILE2's rows get names; **FILE1 stays null until re-imported** (operator's call).
  - **`AllotmentImport.headers` PAID FOR ITSELF TWICE IN ONE SESSION, which is the moment it was built for.** It proved the operator's seven column names verbatim, and it is what turned the `tmName` fix from another guess into a lookup. **When a mapping is provisional, record the document's own header row — then the next person reads instead of guessing.**
- **THE BULK PAN PIVOT — one line per PAN, each application its own block of the REGISTRAR'S OWN columns.** `APPLICATION_NO · BID_CATG · SHARES_APPLIED · AMOUNT_PAID · SHARES_ALLOTED · APPLN_STATUS · REASON_FOR_REJECTION`, ordered **allotted → not allotted → rejected**. Summing a PAN's records into one block was rejected by the operator: it would hide which application was which.
  - **THE THREE OUTCOMES WERE MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED, AND THE SPLIT IS CLEAN.** Across 1.6M rows: `allottedShares > 0` → **379,339**, `= 0` with no reason → **930,694**, `= 0` with a reason → **336,479**, and **not one allotted row carries a reason** — so the three are mutually exclusive and exhaustive. The reasons are real registrar rejections (*"NOT AN EMPLOYEE APPLIED IN EMP CATEGORY"* 186,626 · *"NOT IN BANK"* 100,492). **The operator's model fitted the data exactly, which is why no definition had to be invented.**
  - **EXACT MATCH ON PAN, not the `startsWith` the single search uses.** A pasted list is a list of WHOLE PANs, and a prefix match would pull a stranger's records in under someone else's heading.
  - **SCOPED TO ONE IPO** (operator's call). 18,840 PANs appear in more than one issue, and putting two on one line would make Applied and Allotted figures belonging to different offers read as one total.
  - **MALFORMED AND VALID-BUT-ABSENT PANS ARE EACH REPORTED SEPARATELY, never dropped.** A typo in a pasted column is the likeliest reason a PAN *"is not in the file"*, and *"not in this registrar's data"* is frequently the answer the operator actually wants.
  - **THE SCREEN PAGES AT 50 ROWS WHILE THE CSV CARRIES ALL OF THEM, and that is not polish.** The table is as wide as the widest PAN in the result; **one PAN in the data holds 18 applications**, so 127 columns, and 5,000 rows of that is **635,000 cells no browser renders**. 50 rows is 6,350. The footer says the export is complete so the paging cannot be mistaken for a truncated answer.
  - **ONE BUILDER FOR THE SCREEN AND THE FILE**, so a download cannot be a second opinion about what was shown — the rule `recordWhere` already follows for the record export. CSV headers use the registrar's names because that is what the operator reads; the API stays camelCase.
  - **A POST, not a GET**: 5,000 PANs do not belong in a query string, and a PAN list is not something to leave sitting in an access log.
- **THE FILE STATES `APPLN_STATUS` IN ITS OWN COLUMN AND WE THROW IT AWAY.** `parse-allotment.worker.ts` reads it ONLY as a fallback for files with no shares-allotted column, and JIO's has one — so it never reaches the database. **The status in this report is therefore OUR derivation, not the document's word, which is weaker than this file normally accepts.** Operator's call (2026-10-28): derive it, because the derivation covers all 1.6M existing rows today while capturing the column would only fill rows imported afterwards. **Storing it verbatim is the better answer the day a re-import is cheap** — revisit if both JIO files are ever re-imported.
- ~~**Still open**: *"show all the columns I upload"*~~ **ANSWERED 2026-10-28, by the header row it was waiting for.** JIO's file has **23 columns and we map 12**. These **11 are read and dropped**: `IPO · BID_USERID · BID_BROKER_ID · BID_BROKER · APPLN_TYPE · LOCATION · SCSB_NAME · SCSB_REMARKS · PAYMENT_MODE · CITY · SLNO`. Operator's answer is **none for now**, which is cheap to revisit because the header row is recorded. **`SCSB_NAME` is the one worth revisiting first**: the unblock-request feature emails EVERY sponsor bank on an issue precisely because we do not record which one held the mandate, and that column names it.
- **HOW TO EXERCISE AN API CHANGE BEFORE THE HANDSHAKE WITHOUT A SECOND SERVER — the reusable part.** Compile to `dist-check`, then **construct the compiled service class directly** with a real `PrismaClient` and `{}` for the deps that path does not touch. No Nest, no second HTTP server, no throwaway operator and role to get past the guard, and **only one extra connection pool** — which matters, because two full API instances exhaust Postgres on this box. It found nothing wrong, which is the point: 9 PANs in 12 ms, all three statuses correct, the 18-record PAN pivoted with group order right on every row, CSV at 127 columns = 1 + 18×7 with every line the same width, and the 5,000 cap refusing cleanly.
- **A BROKEN `awk` PRODUCED AN UNVERIFIED ALL-CLEAR ABOUT 7.29 MB OF DELETED FILES.** Checking whether 20 missing tracked images were recoverable, `awk '{print \$3}'` inside a double-quoted shell string died on *"backslash not last character on line"*, so the loop never ran — and the script still printed *"no MISSING lines above = all 20 restorable"*. **A conclusion printed by a line that did not execute is worse than no check**; re-run in node, where there is no shell to escape. (They were all recoverable, confirmed properly: 20 blobs, 0 missing.)
- **AN EMPTY `migrate diff` IS WORTH RUNNING BEFORE A HANDSHAKE, because it tells you which steps you can skip.** This round reported *"This is an empty migration"*, so no `db push` and no `prisma generate` — and therefore nothing the classifier would block and nothing the operator had to run by hand. **Check it first: a code-only handshake is compile, boot-test, restart.**

## A purge that threw into a log nobody reads (2026-10-28)
- **"Delete all data" ANSWERED "Deleted 0 records and 2 import rows" ON AN IPO HOLDING 1,419,960 OF THEM.** Three faults compounded and only the first is about a number.
  - **`purgeRecords` ran `findMany({ take: 50000 })` then `deleteMany({ id: { in: ids } })`, which throws on the FIRST batch**: *"too many bind variables in prepared statement, expected maximum of 32767, received 50000"*. **THE CEILING IS 32,767, not the 65,535 a reading of the wire protocol suggests** — measured: 50,000 throws, 20,000 succeeds in 161 ms.
  - **`enqueue` ENDS IN A `.catch` THAT LOGS AND SWALLOWS.** Right for a background sweep, wrong for anything whose result is reported back — and **`web.config` sets `loggingEnabled="false"`, so the error reached nothing at all.** The method carried on and reported success.
  - **THE IMPORT ROWS WERE DELETED WHETHER OR NOT THE PURGE WORKED**, so a failed delete destroyed the history and left 1,419,960 records orphaned with nothing describing them. **A DESTRUCTIVE OPERATION MUST NEVER REPORT SUCCESS BECAUSE ITS OWN ERROR WAS SWALLOWED**, and the irreversible half must go second.
- **I INHERITED THE `take: 50000` AND CARRIED IT INTO A SECOND PATH.** It is the archive-purge loop extracted into `purgeRecords` on 2026-10-27. Extracting it for reuse was right — the two destructive paths genuinely must not drift — **but it means `doArchive` has had this defect since it was written.** Latent only because archive-then-purge had NEVER RUN (0 `AllotmentArchive` rows), and **`retentionSweep` fires it automatically 60 days after listing**: it would have archived an IPO to gz, failed to purge, and left the database unbounded while reporting success — exactly what that feature exists to prevent. **Two IPOs already exceed the ceiling.** **Extracting code for reuse propagates its defects as faithfully as its behaviour; audit what you are about to share before sharing it.**
- **THE FIX IS A SUBQUERY, NOT A SMALLER NUMBER.** `DELETE FROM "AllotmentRecord" WHERE id IN (SELECT id FROM "AllotmentRecord" WHERE "ipoId" = $1 LIMIT $2)` sends **TWO parameters whatever the batch size**, so the limit stops being reachable rather than being tuned under — and it drops the separate `findMany`, halving the round trips. **Tuning 50,000 down would have worked today and left the same bug one data-growth step away.** `PURGE_BATCH` is 10,000; the SQL strings are literals with the column chosen by branch, never interpolated, and `AllotmentRecord` carries no RLS policy so a raw statement needs no tenant GUC.
- **`enqueueResult` IS `enqueue` THAT LETS THE CALLER SEE A FAILURE.** It serialises identically, rethrows, and **keeps the chain resolved either way** so one failed job cannot wedge every later import behind a rejected promise. Both delete paths use it; the per-IPO one now purges FIRST and deletes the import rows only on success.
- **VERIFIED IN A TRANSACTION THAT WAS ROLLED BACK, which is how a destructive fix gets tested on live data at all.** Three successive batches of exactly 10,000 at 43 / 70 / 114 ms, the `importId` branch valid and returning 0, row count unchanged afterwards. The ORIGINAL failure was isolated the same way: run step 1, then attempt step 2 inside a transaction and throw to roll back — that is what produced the bind-variable message, which `loggingEnabled="false"` had otherwise made unreadable.
- **THREE BADLY-SHAPED GREPS MISREPORTED A GOOD BUILD IN ONE SESSION, and the third is the subtlest.** The `chunks/app/...` regex with no `/` in its class; template-expression literals (`over {CF_LIMIT_MB} MB`) that can never appear contiguously in a bundle; and then **a check for `take: 50000` that matched MY OWN DOCBLOCK describing the removed code.** A comment is not code. **Grep the compiled output and exclude comments when asking whether a fix landed** — `deleteMany-by-id calls left: 0` is the check that means something.
  - Following that false positive up found something real anyway: the patch had **left the old docblock stranded above the new one**, two blocks both opening *"Delete matching records in SLICES"*. **A replacement anchored on a function body leaves its comment behind; check what sits above the thing you replaced.**

## An import count lower than the file, and a job that had died (2026-10-30)
- **THE GAP BETWEEN `totalRows` AND `imported` IS DUPLICATE APPLICATION NUMBERS, AND THERE IS NO PAN VALIDATION — correctly.** Across the eleven JIO files the operator imported, **1,09,99,356 rows landed out of 1,09,99,988 read: 632 skipped**, each one a `(ipoId, applicationNo)` collision with a row an earlier file had already brought in. `createMany({ skipDuplicates: true })` against `@@unique([ipoId, applicationNo])` is the entire mechanism — the parser reads a row, the database declines it, and the two counters disagree by exactly that.
- **DEDUPING ON PAN WOULD BE A BUG, NOT A MISSING FEATURE, and the data says so outright: 5,44,516 rows belong to a PAN that holds more than one application on this issue.** The self-PAN rule is one PUBLIC application per PAN per IPO **plus one per reserved quota**, and a registrar file also carries rejected rows and multiple-application cases. Discarding a second row for the same PAN would delete real applications and quietly shrink the allotment data.
- **BOTH COUNTERS ARE RIGHT AND THEY ANSWER DIFFERENT QUESTIONS** — `totalRows` is what the parser READ, `imported` is what the database ACCEPTED. **The thing to check is that the gap is small, not that it is zero**: 632 in 1.1 crore is 0.006%, i.e. the files overlap slightly, which is what registrar files do. **0 blank application numbers** across all 1,60,71,890 JPL rows, so nothing was skipped for want of a key.
- **A RE-IMPORT THEREFORE CANNOT BACKFILL A COLUMN, which corrects the 2026-10-28 note above.** That section says FILE1's null `tmName` *"stays null until re-imported"* — but `skipDuplicates` skips every row already present, so re-uploading FILE1 would insert **nothing** and fill nothing. **Its rows have to be DELETED first, then the file imported again.** This follows from the unique constraint rather than from a measurement, and it is the kind of claim worth deriving before anyone plans a re-upload.
- **A DEAD PARSE WORKER LEAVES A JOB THAT LOOKS LIVE FOREVER.** FILE14 sat at `importing — 4,04,954 / 9,99,999` with the figure never advancing. **Progress is written BY the worker**, so when the worker dies the last figure it wrote stays on screen exactly as though work were in flight, and **nothing watches for its absence**. Two Expo/Metro dev servers were holding memory at the time, which is the first thing to suspect for a 3–4 GB parse.
  - **THE DISCRIMINATOR IS A PROCESS CHECK, NEVER A LONGER WAIT.** Ask whether a worker exists; from the row alone a stalled figure and a slow one are indistinguishable. Cleanup was the per-IPO delete for the **4,04,954 orphaned rows (6.7 GB)** and the import row marked failed.
  - **STILL OUTSTANDING AND IT NEEDS ELEVATION**: four orphaned uploads, **~298 MB**, in `apps/api/dist/uploads/allotment-tmp`, owned by `IIS APPPOOL/InvestoyardAPI`. Note which directory that is — the same `dist` that holds the LIVE upload store — which is why it is cleared by that exact path and never by anything broader.

## Search the allotment data by application number, and by series range (2026-10-30)
- **MEASURING THE COLUMN FIRST IS WHAT SHAPED BOTH FEATURES, and it is not what the name suggests.** `applicationNo` is **not one series and not a number**: across JPL's 1.60 crore rows it carries **sixteen different widths, from 1 to 16 characters, and 72% of the values contain letters**. The leading non-digit run identifies the INTERMEDIARY, and whether a range means anything at all depends on which one — **GROWW (48,19,084 rows) has a hex tail and cannot be ranged**, while `A124`, `2400`, `UPJPL`, `MB00`, `DHNJPL` and `1700` are sequential.
- **THE RULE THAT MAKES A RANGE CORRECT IS THAT BOTH ENDS ARE THE SAME WIDTH.** Inside one fixed-width series **text order IS numeric order**, which makes a plain `gte`/`lte` both exact and index-backed — measured at **0.036 ms**. Across widths it is **silently wrong**: start `1700001` with end `170010000` drops the first 9,000 rows, because `170000001` sorts before `1700001`. **A mismatched pair is REFUSED with both widths named rather than guessed at** — a wrong count that looks plausible is the expensive failure here, the same reasoning as refusing a carve-out whose rupee reading exceeds the issue size.
- **`packages/shared-types/src/appNoSearch.ts` OWNS THAT RULE, the three match modes and their caps** (21 tests, every fixture a real stored value). Shared for the reason `pollWindow.ts` is: **the screen must state the limits the API enforces**, and a second copy of a limit is a screen that explains a rule the server does not apply.
- **THE RANGE IS NOT A NEW EXPORT — it is a filter in `recordWhere()`**, the one builder the table and the CSV already share, so the file is the rows on screen rather than a second opinion about what the filters meant. Two consequences worth keeping:
  - **THE EXPORT CURSORS ON `applicationNo`, NOT ON `id`, when a range is in force.** **Ordering by one column while paging on another silently repeats and skips rows** — a stream has to page on the column it orders by.
  - **CASE IS NEVER FOLDED.** PAN is uppercased everywhere in this codebase and an application number must NOT be: **47,92,920 JPL rows contain a lowercase letter**, so uppercasing would fail to match every Groww application **while looking like an ordinary empty result**.
- **BULK IS FLAT — one row per number, in pasted order.** Unlike the PAN report there is nothing to pivot, because an application number is unique per IPO where a PAN is not. Malformed and valid-but-absent terms are reported separately, never dropped.
- **CONTAINS IS OFFERED EVERYWHERE AND CAPPED AT 25 RATHER THAN 5,000, MEASURED RATHER THAN ASSUMED.** It cannot use the index, so each term is a parallel seq scan: a **2.3 s floor plus ~280 ms per extra term**, which puts 200 terms at about a minute of held connection. **MY OWN FIRST SUGGESTION WAS WRONG AND THE MEASUREMENT IS WHY IT WAS NOT BUILT** — I proposed a single regex alternation and it is **2–3× WORSE** (50 terms: 45,978 ms against 16,282 ms), so that idea is recorded here as dead rather than left to be re-proposed.
- **TWO FAULTS FOUND RE-READING MY OWN CODE BEFORE HANDING IT OVER, both invisible on a small input.** The bulk prefix attribution was **terms × rows, quadratic**, and prefix allows 5,000 terms — 25 million comparisons; a prefix match is a fixed-length slice and a pasted list has FEW DISTINCT LENGTHS, so it indexes the slice into a map now. And the non-exact query had **no row cap**, so a short prefix could put millions of rows into one JSON response: capped at **20,000 and reported as `truncated`** rather than quietly cut. GROWW returns in **669 ms** instead of hanging.
- **VERIFIED AGAINST THE REAL DATABASE BY CONSTRUCTING THE COMPILED SERVICE DIRECTLY** — no Nest, no second HTTP server, no throwaway operator and role to get past the guard, and only ONE extra connection pool. **48 checks across two suites**: the range total matches a direct SQL count, the exported set is exactly the SQL set in the same order across five batches (20,925 rows), the width guard refuses all three bad shapes, prefix attribution matches an independently computed expectation row for row, and an untouched PAN search is unchanged.
- A known-good range for a smoke test: **`UPJPL00000001` → `UPJPL00001000` returns 728 of 1,000**, so the found-count line and the 272-missing note both appear immediately.

## The port the web build was quietly depending on (2026-10-30)
- **A DEPLOY BAKED SIX IPO PAGES AGAINST 2,639 LIVE, WITH A 51 KB INDEX INSTEAD OF 9.77 MB — AND THE LOG READ `Generating static pages (109/109)` AND EXITED 0.** Only the page-count floor in `scripts/publish-web.mjs` stopped it reaching the live site, and **this is the first time that gate has fired on a real cause rather than in a test.** It is exactly the catastrophic class it was written for: `safeGet` swallows a dead API, every dynamic route collapses to the `no-ipos` placeholder, and the log looks perfect.
- **THE CAUSE WAS NOT OUR CODE, AND THE REAL FINDING IS THAT NOTHING OF OURS LISTENS ON PORT 3000.** `apps/web/.env.production` points every baked page at `API_INTERNAL_URL=http://localhost:3000/api`, and an **unrelated project** (`gmtrade/src/server.js`) had taken 3000 and was answering `/api/health` with a **302 to /login**. Under iisnode the API gets a **NAMED PIPE** and IIS routes to it by `Host` header, **so it has no TCP port to point at at all** — the build had evidently been depending on a forwarder that existed on nobody's disk but the person who started it.
- **FREEING THE PORT DOES NOT FIX IT EITHER: a connection REFUSED and a 302 are swallowed identically.** Something has to ANSWER. That is why the forwarder is now a file — **`scripts/api-forward.js`**, listening on 3000 and forwarding to `127.0.0.1:80` with `Host: newipoapi.finwave.co`.
- **IT PROXIES THE LIVE API AND NEVER STARTS A SECOND ONE.** The obvious alternative, `PORT=3000 node dist/main.js`, is wrong twice over and both halves are already written up here: a second instance opens a **second connection pool** against a box whose live pool already holds ~64 of 100 slots — **that is what P2037 looks like from outside, and it bit twice in one afternoon** — and it starts a **second subscription poller** doing `deleteMany` + `createMany` over the same rows.
- **`--check` EXITS 0 ONLY WHEN THE PORT ANSWERS WITH OUR OWN HEALTH SHAPE** (`checks` plus a numeric `uptimeSec`), because **a 200 proves nothing on this box**: an unbound host falls through to the IIS default site, and another app on the port answers whatever it likes. **Run it before any web deploy.** A second copy of the forwarder notices `EADDRINUSE`, says whether the occupant is really the API, and exits — so a duplicate start is harmless.
- **IT IS RUNNING DETACHED AND WILL NOT SURVIVE A REBOOT UNTIL AN ELEVATED SCHEDULED TASK EXISTS.** Log: `C:/ProgramData/Investoyard/api-forward.log`. Registering the task needs `SeCreateServicePrivilege`, the same wall as `db.ps1 register` — and **the failure printed its own success line**: PowerShell reported the `0x80070005` as a **NON-TERMINATING** error, so the `catch` was skipped and *"registered as the current user"* appeared directly under the exception. **Verified with `Get-ScheduledTask` that the task does not exist.** Third time in one session a command's own account of itself was wrong.
- **The figures in this file rot and these two already have**: the export now builds **2,643 ipo pages** with a **10,001,773-byte index**, against the 2,633 / 9.48 MB recorded on 2026-10-26. The catalogue grows; **re-measure rather than trusting either pair.**

## Match modes on the range, and the build that reached the wrong listener (2026-11-02)
- **THE RANGE TAKES THE SAME THREE MATCH MODES AS THE SINGLE SEARCH, with each range member as the needle** (operator's two points, 2026-11-02). **Starts with COLLAPSES TO ONE INDEX RANGE and that is the whole reason it is affordable**: the collation is byte order and the members are contiguous, so the union of N prefixes is `>= first AND < bumpByte(last)`. Measured over a 1,000-member series: exact (one `IN`) **14 ms**, collapsed prefix **2 ms**, and the OR of 1,000 `startsWith` **92 ms returning identical rows** — which is what proves the collapse is sound rather than merely fast. **Contains cannot collapse**, each term being a parallel seq scan, so it keeps the measured 25-term cap.
  - **MY OWN FIRST PROPOSAL WAS WRONG AND IS RECORDED HERE SO IT IS NOT RE-PROPOSED**: a single regex alternation for contains is **2–3× WORSE** than separate terms (50 terms: 45,978 ms against 16,282 ms).
  - **THE WIDTH GUARD IS WHAT MAKES A RANGE CORRECT, and it is the one rule to keep.** Inside one fixed-width series **text order IS numeric order**, so a plain `gte`/`lte` is both exact and index-backed. Across widths it is **silently wrong**: start `1700001` with end `170010000` drops the first 9,000 rows, because `170000001` sorts before `1700001`. A mismatched pair is **REFUSED with both widths named** rather than guessed at — a wrong count that looks plausible is the expensive failure here.
  - **ENDS ARE RE-PADDED to the stored width when nothing exists at the width typed**, and the resolver is **shared by the table and both exports** so the screen and the file cannot disagree. **The first cut triggered on "the window matched nothing", which is never true** — a width-9 window also admits 13-character values whose first nine characters fall inside it, 20,000 of them. The precise test is *is there anything at the width you typed*, raw SQL on `length("applicationNo")`.
  - **`Export series` IS A SECOND BUTTON, not a mode of the one beside it** (operator's choice). The existing export carries the rows that MATCHED; this lists every application number in the range with its matched number and status, which is **the only way a gap in the series is visible without counting**. **CONTEXT AT THE TOP, TALLY AT THE BOTTOM** — a stream cannot know the counts until it has read every batch, and buffering a million lines to put them first is not an option. 58 tests in `appNoSearch.spec.ts`, every fixture a real stored value; the caps and the refusal wording live there because **the screen must state the limits the API enforces**.
  - **KNOWN LIMIT, written into the source**: in a mixed-width series the string window admits a longer value under the same opening digits, reported as `partial` rather than dropped, so the file keeps agreeing with the count on screen. In a single-width series there are no partials under Exact.
- **THE "Export to Excel" BUTTON ON THAT PAGE HAD NEVER WORKED FROM A BROWSER, AND I COPIED THE BROKEN PATTERN BEFORE MEASURING IT.** It was a plain `<a href>`, deliberately, so a hundreds-of-MB export would stream — but the route is behind `@UseGuards(JwtAuthGuard, PermissionsGuard)` and **that guard reads `Authorization: Bearer` and nothing else**: no cookie, no query token, grepped across `apps/api/src/common`. A link cannot send a header. Probed over loopback: **401 `{"message":"Missing token"}`**. **The five other admin CSV exports already fetched with the header; this was the lone outlier and the lone broken one.** Both buttons now go through one `downloadCsv`. The cost is that the body lands in a blob rather than streaming, traded against delivering nothing; a signed one-time download URL would get the streaming back and is **not built**.
- **THE WEB BUILD FETCHED A DIFFERENT APPLICATION BECAUSE `localhost` RESOLVES TO `::1` FIRST, and the publish gate fired on a real cause for the first time.** Two listeners on port 3000, on two stacks: **`0.0.0.0:3000` (gmtrade) answering `[::1]` with a 302 to `/login`**, and **`127.0.0.1:3000` (our forwarder) answering 200**. `API_INTERNAL_URL` said `localhost`, Node's resolver returns `::1` before `127.0.0.1`, `safeGet` swallowed the 302s exactly as designed, and the export collapsed to **6 ipo pages with a 51,200-byte index**. `out/` came through **byte-identical at 10,001,773 bytes / 2,643 pages** — a failed build cost a retry instead of the 25-minute outage that bought the staged deploy.
  - **WE CANNOT TAKE `::1` WHILE THE OTHER PROCESS HOLDS IT** with a dual-stack socket, so the fix is to stop depending on resolver order: **`API_INTERNAL_URL=http://127.0.0.1:3000/api`**, with the reason written in beside it. **`apps/web/.env.production` IS GITIGNORED (`.gitignore:30` `.env.*`), so that half lives only on this box**; a dated backup sits beside it.
  - **MY OWN `--check` COULD NOT SEE THE FAILURE IT EXISTS TO PREVENT — it probed a hardcoded `127.0.0.1` while the build fetched whatever the env said.** It now reads `API_INTERNAL_URL` out of `.env.production` and probes **EVERY address that host resolves to**, failing if any one of them is a stranger. **A CHECK THAT DOES NOT USE THE SAME ADDRESS AS THE THING IT CHECKS IS NOT A CHECK.** Proved in both directions, because a check that fails for everyone looks identical to a working one: `localhost` → **exit 1** naming `[::1]` and the 302, `127.0.0.1` → exit 0.
  - **A BARE `catch` MADE THAT FIX PASS FOR THE WRONG REASON, which is the same defect again.** `buildTarget` used `fs.readFileSync` in a file that **only requires `http`** — a `ReferenceError`, swallowed, reported as *"no .env.production found"*, and it fell back to the correct address **by luck**. The catch now names what went wrong, because folding *"the file is missing"* into *"this function is broken"* is what hid it.
  - **THE FAILED BUILD POISONS THE FETCH CACHE WITH THE 302s, so it must be cleared AGAIN before the retry** — `.next/cache/fetch-cache` only (16 MB, then 1 MB), never the sibling `webpack` (**419 MB** as of this date). This is the documented poisoning rule meeting its own case: a plain rebuild replays the bad responses and looks perfectly successful.
  - **RUN `node scripts/api-forward.js --check` BEFORE EVERY WEB DEPLOY.** It is now the only thing that can tell a healthy build path from a plausible-looking one.
- **FIFTH BADLY-SHAPED CHECK, AND THIS ONE MATCHED INSIDE A `Warning:` LINE.** The boot test greps for `Cannot find module` and reported FAIL on a clean boot, because **pdfjs-dist prints `Warning: Cannot polyfill DOMMatrix … "Error: Cannot find module 'canvas'`**. Verified rather than assumed: **`canvas` is an OPTIONAL dependency of pdfjs-dist 3.11.174** and nothing in our source or `package.json` references it — we extract PDF text and never render, so the polyfills are not needed. **Pre-existing noise on every boot**, the same family as the Redis version notice. The earlier four: the `chunks/app/...` regex with no `/` in its class, template-expression literals that never appear contiguously in a bundle, a `private async` method missed by `/private seriesStoredWidth\(/`, and a `grep -c` whose `|| echo 0` fired alongside its own `0`.
- **THE DEPLOY, VERIFIED BY CONTENT**: index **10,054,393 bytes** (live byte-identical to disk), **2,643 ipo pages**, **693 carrying real `timesSubscribed`**, gate all six checks OK, both robocopy passes in the right order. The new Range UI is in `chunks/app/admin/allotment/list/page-*.js`.
- **WHY A SECOND API INSTANCE DIES, measured**: **30 modules each provide `PrismaService` and there is no `@Global` PrismaModule**, so a full second instance wants ~30 pools. With the live pool holding 62 of 100 slots that is a `P2037` that **reads exactly like a bug in the change under test**. `connection_limit=1&pool_timeout=30` is enough to prove DI, routing and module init, which is all a boot test is for. Headroom right after a pool restart measured **46 of 100** and settles higher — **re-measure; these figures rot.**
- Two traps that recurred and cost a round trip each: **`/tmp` resolves to `D:\tmp` in Git Bash**, so a `curl -o /tmp/x` and a `node` read of the same path disagree; and **`tsc --outDir dist` left the live upload store intact exactly as documented** — 257 entries, all four subdirectories, `.pii-vault.key` present — which is worth confirming rather than assuming after every handshake.

## ERR_CERT_AUTHORITY_INVALID on the API host is not the server (2026-11-02)
- **RESOLVED 2026-11-03 — it was a FortiGate doing TLS interception, and the operator fixed it on the device.** The section below is the diagnosis as it stood; the full write-up, the one-liner that names the intercepting device, and the explanation I got WRONG twice are under *ERR_CERT_AUTHORITY_INVALID was a FortiGate* near the end of this file.
- **FIVE INDEPENDENT MEASUREMENTS SAY THE ORIGIN IS CORRECT, and my first hypothesis was WRONG.** Reported as a broken image at `https://newipoapi.finwave.co/api/uploads/<uuid>.jpg`. The bound cert for `newipoapi.finwave.co:443` is **`*.finwave.co`, valid to 2026-12-03**; Windows builds a clean **4-link** chain with the intermediate present in `LocalMachine\CA`; **the origin SENDS all four certs on the wire** (not leaf-only, which is what I assumed and tested first); **strict verification against Node's browser-like Mozilla CA set succeeds** with the hostname matched over TLS 1.3; and **that exact image serves `200 image/jpeg 4,276 bytes`**, matching the file on disk byte for byte.
  - **SO THE CAUSE IS CLIENT-SIDE OR PATH-SIDE, and two columns separate the cases.** Expected: **IP `103.107.27.38`** · **issuer `CN=Sectigo Public Server Authentication CA DV R36, O=Sectigo Limited, C=GB`** · **SHA256 `BB01DB83F144B009B59CE2CEE3681D0E4DADA16517F4655FD859074A8448486B`** (SHA1 `D733A18800687BE0EFD903EE432F3AF2396E53C6`). **IP differs → the browser is reaching a different machine** (DNS, the `hosts` file, a VPN). **IP matches and the fingerprint differs → TLS interception**, and the `Issued by` field names the culprit. **Both match → it is Chrome-specific on that machine.** Chrome's own answer to *which machine* is DevTools → Network → the request → Headers → **Remote Address**.
  - **`rejectUnauthorized: false` DOES NOT PROVE TRUST, so the strict connection is the one that counts.** Reading `authorized` off a permissive socket leaves open whether hostname matching ran; connecting with verification ON and succeeding settles chain and hostname together in one measurement.
  - **This box cannot reach the public host at all** (NAT hairpinning), and **curl here is Schannel and will not print the chain** — so the wire chain has to be read with `tls.connect` + `getPeerCertificate(true)`, walking `.issuerCertificate` until a cert points at itself. Only certs actually transmitted appear in that walk, which is the whole measurement.
  - **THE WILDCARD EXPIRES 2026-12-03 — 30 days from this entry — AND IT CANNOT BE SIDESTEPPED BY RETIRING THE HOST.** Partner integrations hold `newipoapi.finwave.co` and the shipped mobile build is compiled against it, so it needs renewing rather than replacing. `*.finwave.co` covers `newipoapi` (one label) and the cert's SANs are exactly `*.finwave.co, finwave.co`.
- **CORRECTION: `newipoapi.finwave.co` AND `newipo.finwave.co` NOW RESOLVE STRAIGHT TO THE ORIGIN — THERE IS NO CLOUDFLARE IN FRONT OF EITHER.** Both answer **103.107.27.38** from 1.1.1.1 and 8.8.8.8, where `finwave.co` itself is **65.254.81.105**. This file said a request to `newipo.finwave.co` was *"still a Cloudflare request"*; that is no longer true, and the whole cached-522 / stale-cache / 100 MB-body analysis therefore **cannot apply to those two hosts either**. The 522 reasoning now describes a path nothing on this box is served over — **re-check which hosts are proxied before reasoning from it.**
- **AN IP CHANGE NEEDS NO CERTIFICATE WORK — THE PFX DOES NOT HAVE TO BE RE-EXPORTED OR RE-IMPORTED** (asked 2026-11-02). Verified from the certificates themselves rather than asserted: both carry **DNS names only, no `iPAddress` SAN** (`DNS:*.finwave.co, DNS:finwave.co` and `DNS:*.investoyard.com, DNS:investoyard.com`), so no address appears anywhere in them and none can invalidate them. **A PFX is only needed to move a PRIVATE KEY to a different machine** — which an IP change is not.
  - **AND NO IIS BINDING IS PINNED TO AN IP, measured rather than read out of `applicationHost.config`** (which needs elevation). Every host answers **identically over loopback and over the LAN address** — `investoyard.com` 200/10,054,393 both ways, `console.finwave.co` 200/1,457, `allotment.investoyard.com` 200/97,646, `www` 301/147 — and **none falls through to the 703-byte default site**, which is what an IP-pinned binding would produce on the address it is not bound to. They are hostname/SNI bindings. **This is the probe that answers the question without elevation: an answer on 127.0.0.1 proves the binding is IP-agnostic.** **TRUE OF THAT MOMENT, AND WORTH DATING: on 2026-11-03 the operator pinned a binding to `192.168.46.88` and loopback stopped answering on 443 within the hour, which I then wrote up here as a correction to this very bullet. It was not a correction - it was a different state. The binding was reverted to All Unassigned the same day and this bullet is accurate again.** See *"All Unassigned" is load-bearing* below.
  - **WHICH IP CHANGES DECIDES EVERYTHING, and the two cases barely overlap.** The **PUBLIC** address `103.107.27.38` **is not bound on this box at all** (NAT; only `192.168.46.88` and loopback are), so the server does not know it and changing it is **a DNS edit and nothing else** — five A records point here: `investoyard.com`, `www.investoyard.com`, `allotment.investoyard.com`, `newipo.finwave.co`, `newipoapi.finwave.co`. The **LAN** address `192.168.46.88` is **statically configured** (`DHCP enabled: No`), so it cannot drift on its own; if someone changes it, the bindings still hold but the **router/firewall port-forward must be repointed**, and `scripts/bind-investoyard-domain.ps1` plus every probe recipe in this file names the old address.
  - **ONE THING AN IP CHANGE DOES NEED A REBUILD FOR, and it is easy to miss**: the admin white-label domain screen prints `103.107.27.38` as the address to tell a partner to point their DNS at, so it is **baked into the shipped bundle** (`out/_next/static/chunks/3553-*.js`) exactly as the canonical host is. Instructional copy, not a runtime dependency — the home page does not even reference that chunk — but it goes stale silently and a partner follows it.
  - **`api.investoyard.com` IS STILL WRONG AND HAS MOVED: it now resolves to 65.254.81.105** (the same parking address as `finwave.co`), not the 103.50.163.230 recorded earlier in this file. Either way it is not this server, so it must be **repointed rather than created** if the API ever moves. **A stale wrong value is worse than none** — it is the one someone would try to fix.
- **THE APP POOL NAMED `newipo.finwave.co` IS STILL ON IIS'S DEFAULT 20-MINUTE IDLE TIMEOUT** — WAS **5186** at 15:52 and 16:21 on 2026-11-02 — while **both Investoyard pools are correctly at `00:00:00` and appear nowhere in that log.** That host is FINWAVE's white-label domain now, so **the 10.6 s cold start documented for our own static pool is being paid by that partner's visitors.** One elevated `appcmd`, and it is the operator's call. **`uptimeSec` on `/api/health` is the cheap liveness tell** — it is `process.uptime()` and `nodeProcessCountPerApplication` is `1`, so a reading that fails to grow between two probes minutes apart means the worker respawned.

## "All Unassigned" is load-bearing, and pinning one binding broke three things (2026-11-03)
- **THE OPERATOR ADDED `192.168.46.88` TO AN IIS BINDING AND IT BROKE LOOPBACK, PORT 80 AND THE WEB BUILD IN ONE MOVE.** Yesterday those bindings were **All Unassigned**; pinning them to the LAN address means **no other local address matches**, and on this box that is not a detail — **the public `103.107.27.38` is not bound locally at all** (NAT), so loopback was the only way in. Measured within minutes of each other:
  - `http://127.0.0.1/api/health` + `Host:` header → **404, naming `C:\inetpub\wwwroot\api\health`** — the IIS DEFAULT site. That is the exact command this file prescribes as *the* API liveness check, so **the documented health check reported the API down while the API was serving perfectly over 443.** That is almost certainly why the day opened with *"API not working"*.
  - `https://127.0.0.1` + SNI → **404 at 315 bytes**, which is **http.sys answering before IIS** — the signature of a host not bound on that address, already documented here for curl and now biting from inside.
  - `https://192.168.46.88` + SNI → **200**. So the site was healthy throughout and only the addresses it answered on had changed.
- **THE BUILD FORWARDER WENT WITH IT, AND THAT IS THE EXPENSIVE HALF.** `scripts/api-forward.js` proxies to `127.0.0.1:80` with the `Host` header, so it began relaying those 404s. `--check` caught it (`ECONNREFUSED`, then a 404 body) — **but `safeGet` swallows a 404 exactly as it swallows a dead API**, so a `npm run deploy` would have baked the no-ipos placeholder across every dynamic route and exited 0. The publish gate would have refused it, i.e. one wasted deploy rather than an outage; the gate earning its keep for the second time in four days.
- **THE REVERT FIXED ALL THREE AT ONCE** — port 80 **200/77 B**, loopback-with-SNI **200**, LAN **200**, `--check` **exit 0**, the forwarder serving the 2.8 MB catalogue in 368 ms. **No code change was needed and the two patches written that morning were rolled back** (`git checkout -- scripts/api-forward.js`, verified byte-identical to HEAD). **When three unrelated things break on the same morning, ask what changed on the box before writing code to tolerate it** — the fix was one IIS checkbox.
- **I "CORRECTED" MY OWN ANSWER TWICE AND BOTH CORRECTIONS WERE WRONG, which is the lesson worth more than the fault.** Asked on 2026-11-02 whether an IP change needs a new PFX, I measured the bindings as **not** IP-pinned and said so. The next morning loopback failed on 443, I concluded the bindings *were* IP-pinned and annotated this file accordingly — and that was describing a state the operator had created an hour earlier. **The original answer was right; it stopped being right because the box changed, not because the measurement was wrong.** A measurement is true of a moment. **Ask what changed before inferring the box has always been that way**, and date a measurement rather than writing it as a property.
- **The caveat the old entry needed**: with **All Unassigned** bindings, loopback and the LAN address both answer and a LAN-IP change costs nothing. With a **pinned** binding, the HTTPS bindings name that address and a change to it breaks them. The certificate half is unaffected either way — **no `iPAddress` SAN, so no PFX work** — which is what that answer was actually about.

## ERR_CERT_AUTHORITY_INVALID was a FortiGate, and it was never ours (resolved 2026-11-03)
- **RESOLVED BY THE OPERATOR ON THE FORTIGATE.** Two days of *"the site is not working"* against a server that was serving a valid, complete, publicly-trusted chain the whole time. **The whole diagnosis is one command**, and it is the thing to reach for before touching a certificate, a binding or a DNS record:

  `$h='newipoapi.finwave.co'; $t=New-Object Net.Sockets.TcpClient($h,443); $s=New-Object Net.Security.SslStream($t.GetStream(),$false,{$true}); $s.AuthenticateAsClient($h); $c=$s.RemoteCertificate; "ISSUER: "+$c.Issuer; "THUMB : "+$c.GetCertHashString(); $s.Close(); $t.Close();`

  It answered `E=support@fortinet.com, CN=FGT60FTK2209DPHB, O=Fortinet` with thumbprint `5EA6...8E6F` — **a FortiGate 60F's own factory CA, presenting its device serial as the CN.** Our own is `Sectigo Public Server Authentication CA DV R36`, SHA1 `D733A18800687BE0EFD903EE432F3AF2396E53C6`. **The trailing `;` is deliberate**: without it a double paste produces `$t.Close()$h='...'` and a parser error, which is exactly what happened and cost a round trip — with it, a double paste simply runs twice.
- **THE MULTI-HOST FORM IS WHAT ACTUALLY DIAGNOSES IT, and running it on one host is what made me wrong.** Against four hosts the answer was **`newipoapi.finwave.co`, `investoyard.com` and `allotment.investoyard.com` ALL intercepted, `finwave.co` NOT** — and `finwave.co` is the one host on a different server (`65.254.81.105`). **My published explanation — that the firewall was choosing per hostname, which is why the site loaded and only the API failed — was FALSE.** The site loads because a top-level page can be **clicked through** ("Advanced → Proceed", remembered per host) while **Chrome never offers that for a background request**: an XHR to the API is blocked outright with no way to accept it. **One host tells you a device is intercepting; four tell you the shape of the policy.**
- **THE SERVER WAS PROVED INNOCENT FIVE WAYS and every one is worth keeping**, because the instinct on a cert error is to reach for the certificate: the bound cert verifies **strictly** against Node's Mozilla CA set with the hostname matched over TLS 1.3 · the origin **sends all four chain certs**, not the leaf alone · **headless Chrome accepted it with checking fully on** · the reported image served **200 `image/jpeg` 4,276 bytes**, matching disk · and external clients completed `/api/tenants` + `/api/ipos` with 200s. **`rejectUnauthorized: false` proves nothing** — reading `authorized` off a permissive socket leaves open whether hostname matching ran; only a connection with verification ON settles chain and hostname together.
- **THIS SERVER'S OWN OUTBOUND IS NOT INSPECTED, which is a useful discriminator** — `google.com` → `WE2`, `raw.githubusercontent.com` → `YR1`, `api.ssllabs.com` → DigiCert, no Fortinet anywhere. So blanket inspection was ruled out from inside, without elevation and without asking anyone.
- **WHAT THIS BOX CANNOT ANSWER, and it is worth knowing the limit**: what the certificate looks like **from the public internet**. The public address is not bound locally, so every local probe either hairpins or reaches the LAN address and sees the certificate IIS actually holds. An outside vantage point (an external TLS checker on our own hostname, publishing off) is the only way, and it was not needed in the end.
- **A traffic reading that looked alarming and was not conclusive**: API distinct clients ran **2-27 per hour on 1 and 2 Nov and 2-4 on the 3rd**. Tempting to read as *"broken for everyone"* — but the static log averages **5 requests per IP**, which is crawler behaviour and not a browser fetching a page with its chunks, so those IPs are not visitors; and part of the drop was **the operator's own blocked admin session**, yesterday's single busiest API client at 652 requests. **A traffic drop is a hypothesis, not a diagnosis.**

## Pulling allotment rows for a list of application-number series (2026-11-03)
- **THE OPERATOR ASKED FOR JPL allotment data across TEN series, then FIVE more, as Excel.** Five workbooks were delivered off the 1,60,71,890 JPL rows. The method is written up because the next request of this shape is one series away, and two of these traps are silent.
- **THE PREFILTER IS KEYED ON LEADING DIGITS AND IT MUST TRACK THE SERIES LIST — THIS IS THE DANGEROUS ONE.** *"Contains the full number"* over 32 lakh numbers is a substring test, and one contains term is a seq scan (~2.3 s floor, measured), so it is not a query. Instead Postgres narrows to rows that could possibly hold an in-range 8- or 9-digit run and the windows are enumerated in Node. For the first ten series that filter was `(4[89])[0-9]{6}` and `(20[01])[0-9]{6}`. **The five new series start 74/75/77/78 and 104/105, so the existing extract was STRUCTURALLY BLIND to them** — reusing it would have returned **zero rows for all five** and looked like a clean answer. Re-running widened the candidate set **4,29,323 → 10,34,069**. **Add a series with a new leading pair and the extract must be re-run.**
- **A ZERO-PADDED FORM IS THE SAME APPLICATION, and treating it as a coincidence loses real rows.** `0000000048345307` is series number **48345307** in the registrar's fixed-width format; `048377540` is 48377540. **2,672 rows across the fifteen series**, and the first cut filed every one as an embedded coincidence because a digit sits beside the window. The fix is to test the **WHOLE VALUE with leading zeros stripped BEFORE** enumerating windows, and register it so the scan cannot emit it twice. Widths reached: 9, 10, 11 and **16** characters.
- **A WINDOW INSIDE A LONGER RUN IS ARITHMETIC, NOT A MATCH.** `DM448511804473` contains `48511804`, but its real digit run is `448511804473`; containing runs measured **9 to 16 digits**. Of 2,87,906 literal contains hits on the first ten series, **2,851 + 156 were real and 2,85,806 were coincidence** — 99.2% noise. The discriminator is whether the window is a **complete digit run** (non-digit neighbours, e.g. `UPJPL48143000`) or whether **the run it sits in strips to it** (`GROWWba049021133`). Both are real; anything else is not. **Shipping them in one sheet would have buried the answer**, so they go in their own workbook with a Read me.
- **ONE SCRIPT FOR EVERY OUTPUT.** The first round had the series list and the classifier copied across two builders, and adding five series meant editing both — the drift this file has paid for repeatedly. One script now writes all four workbooks from one `SERIES` array, with the 8-/9-digit fast-reject spans **derived from it** rather than typed.
- **SheetJS DEFAULTS MAKE A 278 MB FILE.** `compression` and `bookSST` both default to **false**, so the .xlsx zip is STORED and every repeated string is written inline on every row. Setting both: **278 MB → 86.7 MB**. And quantities arriving from CSV as text is not merely size — **Excel will not sum or filter a text column**, which is the first thing the operator tries. Cast them; leave `APPLICATION_NO` as text or the leading zeros that the padded rows exist to show are destroyed.
- **VERIFY THE FINISHED WORKBOOK AGAINST SQL, never against the script that wrote it.** Read the .xlsx back and compare per-series counts with figures SQL computes on its own: 26 checks on the three-file set, 18 on the fourth. It caught nothing in the end, which is the point — the earlier padded-row fault was found by a 15-row discrepancy between two SQL queries, not by reading code. **Two files that should partition must be proved to**: no (application, number) pair in both, and the two row counts summing to every match found.
- **A GAP BETWEEN TWO SERIES IS WORTH NAMING.** As given, `48543001-48543999` and `201000001-201000999` belong to no series. **0 application numbers fall in either**, so nothing was lost — but that is a measurement, not an assumption, and it belongs on the summary sheet rather than in a reply nobody keeps.
- **`psql` IS AT `.tools/pgsql/bin` AND IS THE RIGHT TOOL AT THIS SIZE.** `pg` is not installed and Prisma is the wrong shape for a 1.6-crore scan; `scratchpad/psql.js` wraps it, reading the password out of `apps/api/.env` into `PGPASSWORD` so it never reaches a command line. **Postgres is a service and is reachable with the API pool down**, which is what made this deliverable possible on a morning when the API looked dead.

## The home page rendered a live date during hydration (2026-11-03)
- **THE OPERATOR REPORTED REACT #418 THEN #423 ON EVERY LOAD OF investoyard.com, AND THE CAUSE WAS ONE LINE PRESENT VERBATIM IN TWO FILES**: `const dateLabel = new Date().toLocaleDateString(...)`, evaluated DURING RENDER in a statically exported page. The export bakes the BUILD day - *Mon, 2 Nov* - and the client recomputes *Tue, 3 Nov*.
  - **#423 IS THE EXPENSIVE HALF, NOT #418.** Different text in one node is a mismatch; because it is **outside a Suspense boundary** React then **abandons the whole server-rendered root and re-renders on the client** - throwing away the entire benefit the static export exists for. So this was not a cosmetic console warning.
  - `IpoExplorer2` is the copy on `/`, `TodayStrip` the one on `/homeold`, and `SiteFooter` carried the same shape a third time with `getFullYear()`.
- **IT WAS DIAGNOSED BY PINNING THE CLIENT CLOCK AND BISECTING THE GRANULARITY, which is the reusable part.** `Page.addScriptToEvaluateOnNewDocument` over CDP replaces `window.Date` before any page script runs; then: build instant **0 errors**, +1 minute **0**, +1 hour **0**, +6 hours **0**, **+1 DAY all six back**. Day-granular, which ruled out a countdown and ruled out the stage chips, neither of which drifts on any record between those two days. **A guess about which string is live costs a round trip; pinning the clock answers it.**
- **`istDateLabel()` JOINS `istToday` AND `istClock` IN `ipoClock.ts`** (5 tests), and the weekday and month names are **hand-rolled rather than taken from Intl**: `toLocaleDateString` output depends on the runtime ICU build, and **Node at build time and V8 in a browser are not guaranteed to agree** - which is the last thing a hydration-sensitive string needs. Verified byte-identical to the `en-IN` output it replaces.
- **IT FIXED A SECOND BUG IN THE SAME LINE: the old call read the READER timezone**, so London at 01:00 IST was shown the previous day. **Four lines above it sat a comment saying the reader-calendar bug had been fixed** - and it had been, for the comparisons, never for the visible label. **Third time in this file a comment described a fix that was only half applied.**
- **TWO DIFFERENT REMEDIES ON PURPOSE, because the two cases are not the same thing.** A *Today <date>* label is INHERENTLY LIVE, so it moves to an effect - the pattern `IpoCard` and its `OpensIn` already use and comment as hydration-safe - and `null` on the first render means server and client agree on an empty node. A copyright YEAR is **static text that happens to be derived**, and blanking it for crawlers to dodge a mismatch that can only occur between 1 January and the next build would be the worse trade, so it takes `suppressHydrationWarning`.
- Verified on the published build with the same harness that found it: live clock **0 errors**, and 0 on `/subscription`, `/ipos/open` and `/calendar`. The baked element is now empty - `<b class="h2b-date"></b>` - with no weekday anywhere in the HTML.
- **THE SECOND LITERAL WAS MISSED ON THE FIRST PASS AND CAUGHT BY GREPPING THE SHIPPED BUNDLE.** `HeroBanner` carried the hardcoded label twice, 100 lines apart.
